5.2 Weights and Roots

Our basic strategy in classifying the representations of 51(3; q is to simul-
taneously diagonalize 7r(HI) and 7r(H2). (See Section B.8 for information on
simultaneous diagonalization.) Since HI and H2 commute, 7r(HI) and 7r(H2)
will also commute (for any representation 7r) and so there is at least a chance
that 7r(Hd and 7r(H2) can be simultaneously diagonalized. (Compare Propo-
sition B.13.)

Definition 5.3. If (7r, V) is a representation of 51(3; q, then an ordered pair
f1, = (ml, m2) E C 2 is called a weight for 7r if there exists v i= 0 in V such
that

                                7r(HI)V = mlV,
                                7r(H2)v = m2v.                                 (5.2)
130    5 The Representations of SU(3)

A nonzero vector v satisfying {5.2} is called a weight vector corresponding
to the weight /-l. If /-l = (ml' m2) is a weight, then the space of all vectors v
satisfying {5.2} {including the zero vector} is the weight space correspond-
ing to the weight /-l. The multiplicity of a weight is the dimension of the
corresponding weight space.

    Thus, a weight is simply a pair of simultaneous eigenvalues for 7r(Hd and
7r(H2). (See Section B.8 for a discussion of simultaneous eigenvectors and
eigenvalues.) It is easily shown that equivalent representations have the same
weights and multiplicities.

Proposition 5.4. Every representation of 51(3; q has at least one weight.

Proof. Since we are working over the complex numbers, 7r(Hd has at least one
eigenvalue ml E C. Let W c V be the eigenspace for 7r(Hd with eigenvalue
mI. Since [HI, H2J = 0, 7r(H2) commutes with 7r(Hd, and, so, by Proposition
B.4, 7r(H2) must map W into itself. Thus, 7r(H2) can be viewed as an operator
on W. Then, the restriction of 7r(H2) to W must have at least one eigenvector
w with eigenvalue m2 E C and w is a simultaneous eigenvector for 7r(HI) and
7r(H2) with eigenvalues ml and m2.                                         0

     Now, every representation 7r of 51(3; C) can be viewed, by restriction, as a
representation of the subalgebra {HI, Xl, Yd ~ 51(2; q. Note that even if 7r
is irreducible as a representation of 51(3; C), there is no reason to expect that
it will still be irreducible as a representation of the subalgebra {HI,XI,Yd·
Nevertheless, 7r restricted to {HI, Xl, Yd must be some finite-dimensional
representation of 51(2; C). The same reasoning applies to the restriction of 7r
to the subalgebra {H2' X 2, Y 2 }, which is also isomorphic to 51(2; q.
     Now, recall Theorem 4.12, which tells us that in any finite-dimensional
representation of 51(2; q, irreducible or not, all of the eigenvalues of 7r(H)
must be integers. Theorem 4.12 has the following corollary.

Corollary 5.5. If 7r is a representation of 51(3; q, then all of the weights of
7r are of the form


with ml and m2 being integers.

Proof. Apply Theorem 4.12 to the restriction of 7r to {HI, Xl, Y I } and to the
restriction of 7r to {H2' X 2, Y 2 }.                                         0

    Our strategy now is to begin with one simultaneous eigenvector for 7r(Hd
and 7r(H2) and then to apply 7r(Xi ) or 7r(1'i) and see what the effect is. The
following definition is relevant in this context.

Definition 5.6. An ordered pair a = (aI, a2) E C 2 is called a root if

 1. al and a2 are not both zero, and
                                                     5.2 Weights and Roots    131

 2. there exists a nonzero Z E 51(3; q such that

                                   [H 1, Z] = a1Z,
                                   [H2, Z] = a2Z.

   The element Z is called a root vector corresponding to the root o.

    Condition 2 in the definition says that Z is a simultaneous eigenvector
for ad H 1 and ad H 2. This means that Z is a weight vector for the adjoint
representation with weight (a1, a2). Thus, taking into account Condition 1,
we may say that the roots are precisely the nonzero weights of the adjoint
representation. Corollary 5.5 then tells us that for any root, both a1 and a2
must be integers, which we can also see directly in (5.3). The commutation
relations (5.1) tell us what the roots for 51(3; q are. There are six roots:

                                      a    Z
                                   (2, -1) Xl
                                   (-1,2) X 2
                                    (1,1) X3                                 (5.3)
                                   (-2,1) Y1
                                   (1, -2) Y2
                                  (-1,-1) Y3

Note that H1 and H2 are also simultaneous eigenvectors for ad H1 and ad H2 ,
but they are not root vectors because the simultaneous eigenvalues are both
zero. Since the vectors in (5.3) together with H1 and H2 form a basis for
51(3; q, it is not hard to show that the roots listed in (5.3) are the only roots
(Exercise 1). These six roots form a "root system," conventionally called A 2 .
For more information, see Chapters 6, 7, and 8.
    It is convenient to single out the two roots corresponding to Xl and X 2
and give them special names:

                                 01=(2,-1),
                                 02 = (-1,2).                                (5.4)

The roots 01 and 02 are called the positive simple roots. They have the
property that all of the roots can be expressed as linear combinations of 01
and 02 with integer coefficients, and these coefficients are (for each root)
either all greater than or equal to zero or all less than or equal to zero. This
is verified by direct computation:

                              (2, -1)
                              (-1,2)
                              (1, 1)
                              (-2,1)
                              (1, -2)    -02,
                             (-1,-1) = -01-02.
132     5 The Representations of SU(3)

(The decision to designate 01 and 02 as the positive simple roots is arbitrary;
any other pair of roots with similar properties would do just as well. We simply
choose one set of positive simple roots and hold to that choice throughout the
chapter. See Section 6.8.)
    The significance of the roots for the representation theory of 51(3; <C) is
contained in the following lemma. Although its proof is very easy, this lemma
plays a crucial role in the classification of the representations of 51(3; <C). Note
that this lemma is the analog of Lemma 4.10, which was the key to the clas-
sification of the representations of 51(2; <C).

Lemma 5.7. Let 0 = (al,a2) be a root and Za a corresponding root vector
in 51(3; <C). Let 7r be a representation of51(3;<C), fl, = (ml,m2) a weight for 7r,
and v -lOa corresponding weight vector. Then,

                       7r(Hl)7r(Za)v = (ml + al)7r(Za)v,
                       7r(H2)7r(Za)v = (m2 + a2)7r(Za)v.

Thus, either 7r(Za)v = 0 or 7r(Za)v is a new weight vector with weight



Proof. The definition of a root tells us that we have the commutation relation
[HI, Za] = alZa' Thus,

                  7r(Hl)7r(Za)v = (7r(Za)7r(Hd + al7r(Za)) v
                                = 7r(Za)(mlv) + al7r(Za)V
                                = (ml + al)7r(Za)V.
A similar argument allows us to compute 7r(H2)7r(Za)v.                            o


5.3 The Theorem of the Highest Weight
We see then that if we have a representation with a weight fl, = (ml' m2), then
by applying the root vectors Xl, X 2 , X 3 , Yl , Y2 , and Y3 , we can get some new
weights of the form fl, + 0, where 0 is the root. Of course, some of the time,
7r(Za)V will be zero, in which case fl, + 0 is not necessarily a weight. In fact,
since our representation is finite dimensional, there can be only finitely many
weights, so we must get zero quite often. By analogy to the classification of the
representations of 51(2; C), we would like to single out in each representation
a "highest" weight and then work from there. The following definition gives
the "right" notion of highest.

Definition 5.8. Let 01 = (2,-1) and 02 = (-1,2) be the roots introduced
in (5.4). Let fl,l and fl,2 be two weights. Then, fl,l is higher than fl,2 (or,
equivalently, fl,2 is lower than fl,l) if fl,l - fl,2 can be written in the form
                                  5.3 The Theorem of the Highest Weight        133




with a 2': 0 and b 2': O. This relationship is written as fLl ~ fL2 or fL2 ~ fLl.
   If 7r is a representation of 51(3; C), then a weight fLo for 7r is said to be a
highest weight if for all weights fL of 7r, fL ~ fLo·

    Note that the relation of "higher" is only a partial ordering; that is, one
can easily have fLl and fL2 such that fLl is neither higher nor lower than fL2·
For example, QI - Q2 is neither higher nor lower than o. This, in particular,
means that a finite set of weights need not have a highest element (e.g., the
set {O, QI - Q2} has no highest element). Note also that the coefficients a and
b do not have to be integers, even if both fLl and fL2 have integer entries. For
example, (1,0) is higher than (0,0) since (1,0) = ~QI + !Q2.
    We are now ready to state the main theorem regarding the irreducible
representations of 51 (3; C), commonly called the theorem of the highest weight.

Theorem 5.9.

 1. Every irreducible representation 7r of 51(3; C) is the direct sum of its weight
    spaces; that is, 7r(Hd and 7r(H2) are simultaneously diagonalizable in ev-
    ery irreducible representation.
 2. Every irreducible representation of 51(3; C) has a unique highest weight
    fLo, and two equivalent irreducible representations have the same highest
    weight.
 3. Two irreducible representations of 51(3; C) with the same highest weight
    are equivalent.
 4· If 7r is an irreducible representation of 51(3; C), then the highest weight fLo
     of 7r is of the form


    with ml and m2 being non-negative integers.
 5. If ml and m2 are non-negative integers, then there exists an irreducible
    representation 7r of51(3;C) with highest weight fLo = (ml,m2).

    An ordered pair (mIl m2) with ml and m2 being non-negative integers is
called a dominant integral element. Theorem 5.9 tell us that the highest
weight of each irreducible representation of 51(3; C) is a dominant integral
element and, conversely, that every dominant integral element occurs as the
highest weight of some irreducible representation. Since (1,0) = ~QI +!Q2 and
(0,1) = !QI +~Q2' we see that every dominant integral element is higher than
zero. However, if fL has integer coefficients and is higher than zero, this does
not necessarily mean that fL is dominant integral. (For example, QI = (2, -1)
is higher than zero but is not dominant integral.)
    Figure 5.1 shows the roots and dominant integral elements for 51(3; C). This
picture is made using the obvious basis for the space of weights; that is, the
x-coordinate is the eigenvalue of HI and the y-coordinate is the eigenvalue
of H 2 . Once we have introduced the Weyl group (Section 5.6), we will see
134    5 The Representations of SU(3)




                                  "2

                                       1\
                                ""- N L                 -+-

                                       V \' i'-... "I    ml



                                             \

  Fig. 5.1. Roots and dominant integral elements for 51(3; q (in obvious basis)


the same picture (Figure 5.2) rendered using a Weyl-invariant inner product,
which will give a more geometric view of the situation.
    Note the parallels between this result and the classification of the ir-
reducible representations of 51(2; C): In each irreducible representation of
51(2; q, 7f(H) is diagonalizable, and there is a largest eigenvalue of 7f(H).
Two irreducible representations of 51(2; q with the same largest eigenvalue
are equivalent. The highest eigenvalue is always a non-negative integer, and,
conversely, for every non-negative integer m, there is an irreducible represen-
tation with highest eigenvalue m.
    Note, however, that in the classification of the representations of 51(3; C),
the notion of "highest" does not mean what we might have thought it should
mean; that is, (mI' m2) !:::: (nI' n2) does not mean mI ~ nI and m2 ~ n2,
as we might have guessed. (For example, the weight (1,1) is higher than the
weights (-1,2) and (2, -1).) Nevertheless, the condition on which weights
can be highest weights is the obvious one: mI and m2 must be non-negative
integers.
    It is possible to obtain much more information about the irreducible rep-
resentations besides the highest weight. For example, we have the following
formula for the dimension of the representation with highest weight (mI' m2).

Theorem 5.10. The dimension of the irreducible representation with highest
weight (mI' m2) is
                      1
                      "2(mI + 1)(m2 + 1)(mI + m2 + 2).

    See Humphreys (1972, Section 24.3). (Humphreys refers to 51(3; q as A2')
We will not prove this formula here. It is a consequence of the Weyl character
formula, which is discussed in the context of general semisimple Lie algebra,s
in Sections 7.4 and 7.6.
                                                    5.4 Proof of the Theorem     135

5.4 Proof of the Theorem
It will take us some time to prove Theorem 5.9. The proof will consist of a
series of propositions.
Proposition 5.11. In every irreducible representation (7f, V) of51(3; q, 7f(Hd
and 7f(H2) can be simultaneously diagonalized; that is, V is the direct sum of
its weight spaces.
Proof. Let W be the direct sum of the weight spaces in V. Equivalently, W is
the space of all vectors w E V such that w can be written as a linear combi-
nation of simultaneous eigenvectors for 7f(Hd and 7f(H2). Since (Proposition
5.4) 7f always has at least one weight, W =I=- {O}.
    On the other hand, Lemma 5.7 tells us that if Zo. is a root vector corre-
sponding to the root Q, then 7f(Zo.) maps the weight space corresponding to J.t
into the weight space corresponding to J.t + Q. Thus, W is invariant under the
action of all of the root vectors, namely under the action X l ,X2 ,X3 , Yll Y2 ,
and Y3 . Since W is certainly invariant under the action of Hl and H 2 , W is
invariant under all of 51(3; q. Thus, by irreducibility, W = V.               D
Definition 5.12. A representation (7f, V) of 51(3; q is said to be a highest
weight cyclic representation with weight J.to = (ml' m2) if there exists
v =I=- 0 in V such that
 1. v is a weight vector with weight J.to,
 2. 7f(Xl )v = 7f(X2 )v = 0,
 3. the smallest invariant subspace of V containing v is all of V.
The vector v is called a cyclic vector for 7f.
Proposition 5.13. Let (7f, V) be a highest weight cyclic representation of
51(3; q with weight J.to. Then,
 1. 7f has highest weight J.to and
 2. the weight space corresponding to the highest weight J.to is one dimensional.
   Before turning to the proof of this proposition, let us record a simple
lemma that applies to arbitrary Lie algebras and which will be useful also in
the setting of general semisimple Lie algebras.
Lemma 5.14. Suppose that g is any Lie algebra and that 7f is a representation
ofg. Suppose that Xl' ... ' Xm is an ordered basis for g as a vector space. Then,
any expression of the form
                             7f(Xi1 )7f(Xi2 )·· ·7f(XiN ),                     (5.5)
can be expressed as .a linear combination of terms of the form
                       7f(Xm )k"'7f(Xm _d k" , - l • • • 7f(Xd k1              (5.6)
where in each term kl + ... + k m ::::; N. Here, the k i 's are non-negative integers
(zero is allowed!).
136      5 The Representations of SU(3)

Proof. Let us think about how this works in the case N ::; 2. If N = 1, there
is nothing to do: Any expression of the form 71'(Xi ) is of the form (5.6) with
k i = 1 and all the other k j 's equal to zero. If N = 2, we consider an expression

Proposition 5.15. Every irreducible representation of 51(3; q is a highest
weight cyclic representation, with a unique highest weight fLo.
Proof. Uniqueness is immediate, since by the previous proposition, fLo is the
highest weight, and two distinct weights cannot both be highest.
    We have already shown that every irreducible representation is the direct
sum of its weight spaces. Since the representation is finite dimensional, there
can be only finitely many weights. It follows that there must exist a weight
fLo such that there is no weight fL -=I=- fLo with fL C::: fLo. This says that there is
no weight higher than fLo (which is not the same as saying that fLo is highest).
However, if there is no weight higher than fLo, then for any nonzero weight
vector v with weight fLo, we must have

5.6 The Weyl Group

There is an important symmetry to the representations of 51(3; C) involving
something called the Weyl group. (Our treatment will follow the compact
group approach, which is apparently different from the Lie algebra approach,
but ultimately equivalent to it.) To understand the idea behind the Weyl group
symmetry, let us observe that the representations of 51(3; C) are, in a certain
sense, invariant under the adjoint action of SU(3). What I mean by this is the
following. Let 7r be a finite-dimensional representation of 51(3; q acting on a
vector space V and let IT be the associated representation of SU(3) acting on
the same space. For any A E SU(3), we can define a new representation 7rA of
51(3; C), acting on the same vector space V, by setting



Since the adjoint action of A on 51(3; q is a Lie algebra automorphism, this
is, again, a representation of 51(3; C). This new representation is easily seen
to be equivalent to the original representation; direct calculation shows that
IT(A) is an intertwining map between (7r, V) and (7rA, V). We may say, then,
                                                    5.6 The Weyl Group      143

that the adjoint action of SU(3) is a symmetry of the set of equivalence classes
of representations of 51(3; C).
    Now, we have analyzed the representations of 51(3; <C) by simultaneously
diagonalizing the operators 7r(Hd and 7r(H2). Of course, this means that any
linear combination of 7r(Hl) and 7r(H2) is also simultaneously diagonalized. So,
what really counts is the two-dimensional subspace b of 51(3; C) spanned by Hl
and H 2 . (This space is called a Cartan subalgebra of 51(3; C). See Chapter
6 for more information.) Now, in general, the adjoint action of A E SU(3)
will not preserve the space b and so the equivalence of 7r and 7rA does not (in
general) tell us anything about the weights of 7r. However, there are elements
A in SU(3) for which AdA does preserve b. These elements make up the Weyl
group for SU(3) and (as we shall see below) give rise to a symmetry of the
set of weights of any representation 7r. So, we may say that the Weyl group is
the "residue" of the adjoint symmetry of the representations (discussed in the
previous paragraph) that is left after we focus our attention on the subspace
b of 51(3; C).
Definition 5.20. Let b be the two-dimensional subspace of51(3; C) spanned by
Hl and H 2 • Let Z be the subgroup ofSU(3) consisting of those A E SU(3) such
that AdA (H) = H for all H E b. Let N be the subgroup of SU(3) consisting
of those A E SU(3) such that AdA (H) is an element of b for all H in b·

   It is a straightforward exercise (Exercise 8) to verify that Z and N are
actually subgroups of SU(3) and to verify that Z is a normal subgroup of N.
This leads us to the definition of the Weyl group.

Definition 5.21. The Weyl group of SU(3), denoted W, is the quotient
group NjZ.

   We can define an action of W on b as follows. For each element w of W,
choose an element A of the corresponding equivalence class in N. Then for H
in b we define the action w . H of w on H by



To see that this action is well defined, suppose B is another element of the
same equivalence class as A. Then B = AC with C E Z and, thus,



by the definition of Z. It is easily seen that W is isomorphic to the group of
linear transformations of b that can be expressed as AdA for some A E N.
    The following proposition will allow us to compute W explicitly.

Proposition 5.22. The group Z consists precisely of the diagonal matrices
inside SU(3), namely the matrices of the form
144    5 The Representations of SU(3)


                                                                          (5.15)


for (j and ¢ in R The group N consists of precisely those matrices A E SU(3)
such that for each k = 1,2,3, there exist l E {I, 2, 3} and (j E lR such that
Aek = eieel. Here, e1,e2, e3 is the standard basis for «::3.
    The Weyl group W = N / Z is isomorphic to the permutation group on
three elements.

Proof. Suppose A is in Z, which means that A commutes with all elements
of I). Then, certainly, A must commute with HI. Now, the matrix HI has
eigenvalues 1, -1, and O. The corresponding eigenspaces are the span of e1,
the span of e2, and the span of e3. Since A commutes with HI, it must preserve
each of these eigenspaces (Proposition B.4). This means that Aek must be a
multiple of ek for each k = 1,2,3. This is the same as saying that A is diagonal.
If A is also to be unitary and have determinant 1 then it must be of the form
in the proposition. Conversely, any matrix of the form (5.15) does indeed
commute not only with HI but also with H2 and, thus, with every element of
I). So, Z consists precisely of the form (5.15).
     Now, suppose that A is in N. Then, AH1A -1 must be in I) and there-
fore must be diagonal. Now, HI has eigenvectors el, e2, and e3 with distinct
eigenvalues 1, -1, O. Then, AHA- 1 will have eigenvectors Ae1, Ae2, and Ae3
with the same eigenvalues, 1, -1, O. Since the eigenvalues of AHA -1 are dis-
tinct, the only eigenvectors it has are multiples of Ae1, multiples of Ae2, and
multiples of Ae3' (This would not be the case if AHA -1 had a repeated eigen-
value.) On the other hand, AHA- 1 is diagonal, which means it has e1, e2, and
e3 as eigenvectors. The only way these two descriptions of the eigenvectors
of AHA -1 can agree is if each Aek is a constant multiple of some el. The
constant must have absolute value 1 if A is unitary.
     Conversely, suppose A is in SU (3) and A takes each ek to eie el. Then, the
eigenvectors for AH1A- 1 will still be e1, e2, and e3 (but with the eigenvalues
possibly in a different order) and so AH1A- 1 will be diagonal. Furthermore,
since HI has trace zero, AH1 A -1 will also have trace zero. However, I) consists
of all diagonal 3 x 3 matrices with trace zero, and so this shows that AH1A-1
is in I). The same argument shows that AH2A- 1 is in I).
     Now, let us think about what AdA looks like as a linear transformation of
I), for A in N. For k = 1,2,3, let O'(k) be the element of {I, 2, 3} such that A
maps ek to a multiple of eu(k). Since A is invertible, the map k --+ O'(k) must
be a permutation of the set {I, 2, 3} . Now each element H of I) is a diagonal
matrix, which means that H has e1, e2, and e3 as eigenvectors; the diagonal
entries of H are precisely the corresponding eigenvalues AI, A2, and A3. Then,
AHA- 1 has eu(k) as an eigenvector with eigenvalue Ak. This means that the
diagonal entry of H that was originally in the kth spot of H is now in the
O'(k)th spot.
                                                     5.6 The Weyl Group       145

     We see, then, that A acts on b by permuting the diagonal entries of each
H E b according to the permutation 0". Thus, the group of linear transforma-
tions of b of the form AdA, A E N, is isomorphic to the permutation group
on three elements. This group of linear transformations is (isomorphic to) the
Weyl group W = N/Z.
     Note that although each entry of A maps each ek to some constant multiple
e i1h of eU(k)' the action of AdA on b depends only on the value of O"(k) and
not on the constants e iih . This reflects that if one multiplies any A E N on
the right by some B E Z, then AdAB(H) = AdAAdB(H) = AdA(H), since
by the definition of Z, the adjoint action of B on b is just the identity.   D

    In the case of SU(3), it is possible to identify the Weyl group with a certain
subgroup of N, instead of as the quotient group N / Z. See Exercise 9. Exercise
10 asks one to verify by direct calculation that the action of a particular
element A of N is as described in the above proof.
    We want to show that the Weyl group is a symmetry of the weights of any
finite-dimensional representation of 51(3; C). To understand this, we need to
adopt a less basis-dependent view of the weights. We have defined a weight as a
pair (ml' m2) of simultaneous eigenvalues for 7r(HI) and 7r(H2). However, if a
vector v is an eigenvector for 7r(Hd and 7r(H2) then it is also an eigenvector for
7r(H) for any element H of the space b spanned by HI and H 2. Furthermore,
the eigenvalues must depend linearly on H since if Hand J are any two
elements of band 7r(H)v = AIV and 7r(J) = A2V, then

                      7r(aH + bJ)v = (a7r(H) + b7r(J))v
                                     = (aAI + bA2)V.
So, we may make the following basis-independent notion of a weight.

Definition 5.23. Let b be the subspace of 51(3; q spanned by HI and H2 and
let 7r be a finite-dimensional representation of 51(3; q acting on a vector space
V. A linear functional JL E b* is called a weight for 7r if there exists a nonzero
vector v in V such that
                                 7r(H)v = JL(H)v
for all H in b. Such a vector v is called a weight vector with weight JL.
    So, a weight is just a collection of simultaneous eigenvalues of all the
elements H of b, which, as we have noted, must depend linearly on Hand
which, therefore, define a linear functional on b. Since HI and H2 span b, the
linear functional JL is determined by the value of JL(Hd and JL(H2), and thus
our new notion of weight is equivalent to our old notion of a weight as just a
pair of simultaneous eigenvalues of 7r(Hd and 7r(H2). The reason for adopting
this basis-independent approach is that the action of the Weyl group does not
preserve the basis {HI, H 2} for b.
    The Weyl group is (or may be thought of as) a group of linear transforma-
tions of b. This means that W acts linearly on b, and we denote this action as
146    5 The Representations of SU(3)

w . H. We can define an associated action on the dual space ~* as in the defi-
nition of the dual representation in Chapter 4. Thus, for fL E ~* and w E W,
we define w . fL to be the element of ~* given by

                           (W· fL) (H) = fL(W- 1 . H).                     (5.16)

    We now come to the main point of the Weyl group from the point of view
of repre~entation theory, namely that the weights of any representation are
invariant under the action of the Weyl group.

Theorem 5.24. Suppose that 7r is any finite-dimensional representation of
51(3; q and that fL E ~* is a weight for 7r. Then, for any wE W, W . fL is also
a weight of 7r, and the multiplicity of W . fL is the same as the multiplicity of
fL·
Proof. Suppose that fL is a weight for a representation (7r, V) of 51(3; q and
suppose that v is a weight vector with weight fL. Then, let II be the associated
representation of the simply-connected group SU(3) and consider the vector
II(A)v, for A E N. We want to show that II(A)v is, again, a weight vector.
So, we compute

                   7r(H)II(A)v = II(A)II(A)-l7r(H)II(A)v
                                 = II(A)7r(A -1 H A)v
                                 = fL(A -1 H A)II(A)v.
Here, we have used that A is in N, which guarantees that A -1 H A is, again,
in~. However, A- 1 HA is nothing but w- 1 . H, where w is the Weyl group
element represented by A. Thus, by (5.16), fL(A- 1 HA) = (w· fL)(H). This
shows that II(A)v is, again, a weight vector, with weight w . fL and thus W . fL
is, again, a weight for (7r, V). The same sort of reasoning shows that II(A)
is an invertible map of the weight space associated to the weight fL onto the
weight space with weight W· fL, whose inverse is II(A)-l. This means that the
two weight spaces have the same dimension and, therefore, fL and w . fL have
the same multiplicity.                                                        0

    Note that since the roots are nothing but the nonzero weights of the adjoint
representation, this result tells us that the roots are invariant under the action
of the Weyl group. In order to visualize the action of the Weyl group, it is
convenient to identify ~* with ~ by means of an inner product on ~ that is
invariant under the action of the Weyl group. Recall that ~ is a subspace of
the space of diagonal matrices, and we use on the space of diagonal matrices
the inner product obtained by identifying with (:3 in the obvious way. (This
inner product is, if one prefers, the restriction to the diagonal matrices of the
Hilbert-Schmidt inner product (A, B) = trace(A* B). See Section B.6.) Since
the Weyl group acts by permuting the diagonal entries, this inner product
(restricted to the subspace ~) is preserved by the action of W.
                                                       5.6 The Weyl Group     147

5.7 Weight Diagrams
In this section, we consider weight diagrams for 51(3; C) (i.e., pictures of the
weights of various representations of51(3;C)). (These weight diagrams should
not be confused with Dynkin diagrams, which are discussed in Chapter 8.)
The action of the Weyl group is critical to understanding which weights arise
in a representation with a given highest weight /-La.
Definition 5.25. Let VI,"" vn be a finite collection of points in a vector
space V. The convex hull of VI, ... ,Vn is the set of all vectors in V that can
be expressed as
                        CI V I + C2V2 + ... + CnVn'
where C1, ... ,Cn are non-negative real constants satisfying Cl + ... + Cn = 1.

    Equivalently, the convex hull is the smallest convex subset of V containing
all of the points VI, ... , Vn .
    To make the weight diagrams, we make use of the following result. As in
(5.17), we regard the weights (and so also the roots) as elements of ~.
Theorem 5.26. Suppose that 7r is an irreducible representation of 51(3; C)
with highest weight /-La. Then, an element /-L of ~ is a weight of the represen-
tation 7r if and only if the following two conditions are satisfied:
150     5 The Representations of SU(3)

 1. J.l is contained in the convex hull of the orbit of J.lo under the Weyl group.
 2. J.lo - J.l is expressible as a linear combination of al and a2 with integer
    coefficients.

     Let us think first about Condition 1. The Weyl group of SU(3) has six
elements and the orbit of a "generic" point in ~ will consist of six points.
These six points will form the vertices of a hexagon. The simplest way to see
how this will work out is first to apply to J.lo a reflection (say, about the line
perpendicular to ad and then to apply 120 0 clockwise and counterclockwise
rotations to the resulting pair of points to get a total of six points. Suppose,
however, that one starts with a dominant integral element J.lo that is on the
edge of the set of all dominant integral elements (these are the elements of
the form (ml,O) or (0, m2) in our old view of the weights). Then, J.lo is left
invariant by either the reflection about the line perpendicular to al or by the
reflection about the line perpendicular to a2. In that case, the orbit of J.lo is a
triangle (unless J.lo = 0, in which, case the orbit is just a single point). Finally,
the convex hull of the orbit of J.lo will be a filled-in triangle or hexagon (or a
single point if J.lo = 0).
     Let us think now about Condition 2. Condition 2 implies that J.l must be
an integral element (Le., that (J.l, HI) and (J.l, H 2) are integers), since J.lo, aI,
and a2 all have this property. However, not every integral element will satisfy
Condition 2. Suppose, for example, that J.lo = (1,0) and J.l = (0,0). Then,
J.lo - J.l = ~al + ~a2 and the coefficients are not integral. So, (0,0) is not a
weight of the irreducible representation with highest weight (1,0). In most
cases, there will be integral elements contained in the convex hull of the orbit
of J.lo that are not weights of the representation with highest weight J.lo.
     I will give only the main idea of the proof of Theorem 5.26. It is not too
hard to show that Conditions 1 and 2 are both necessary conditions for the
weights of the representation with highest weight J.lo. See Exercises 13 and
14. Showing that the conditions are sufficient is an 51(2; q argument and
makes use of the fact that there can be no "gaps" in the eigenvalues of H in
a finite-dimensional representation of 51(2; C): If a non-negative integer k is
an eigenvalue for H in some representation, then so are k - 2, k - 4, ... , -k.
(One starts with the orbit of J.lo under the Weyl group and then uses the just-
mentioned result, applied to various 51(2; q subalgebras of 51(3; q, to "fill in"
all the elements satisfying Conditions 1 and 2.)
     Theorem 5.26 tells us which weights occur in a given representation of
51(3; q but not what the multiplicities of the weights are. It can be shown
that the multiplicities obey the following simple pattern. The weights occur
in "rings" in which the rings toward the outside are hexagons and the rings
toward the inside are triangles. The weights in the outermost ring have mul-
tiplicity 1. The multiplicities then increase by 1 each time one moves inward
one ring, until the rings become triangles, at which point the multiplicities
stabilize. The situation for the multiplicities in other semisimple Lie algebras
is more complicated-see Section 7.6.

    Figures 5.4, 5.5, and 5.6 show the weights and multiplicities for three

spectively. In each figure, a black dot indicates a weight of the representation,

(1,2). The dimensions of these representations are 15, 27, and 15, respectively,

Semisimple Lie Algebras




In this chapter, we will consider a class of Lie algebras (the complex semisim-
pIe ones) that are sufficiently similar to 51(3; C) that their representations can
be described, similarly to 51(3; C), by a "theorem of the highest weight." We
will not come to the representations themselves until the next chapter; in this
chapter, we develop the structures needed to state the theorem of the highest
weight. Although this chapter could be understood simply as a description
of the structure of semis imp Ie Lie algebras, without any mention of repre-
sentation theory, I think it is helpful to have the representations in mind.
The representation theory, especially in light of our experience with 51(3; C),
motivates the notions of Cartan subalgebras, roots, and the Weyl group.
    We will give three equivalent characterizations of semisimple Lie algebras
(and there are several other commonly used ones). The first characterization is
the one that we will take as our definition and which presumably accounts for
the term "semisimple": A semisimple Lie algebra is one which is isomorphic
to a direct sum of simple Lie algebras. The second characterization is that
a complex Lie algebra is semisimple if and only if it is isomorphic to the
complexification of the Lie algebra of a compact simply-connected group. This
characterization shows, for example, that 51(n; <C) ~ 5u(n)c is semisimple. The
third characterization is that a Lie algebra 9 is semisimple if and only if it
has the complete reducibility property, that is, if and only if every finite-
dimensional representation of 9 decomposes as a direct sum of irreducibles.
    Before getting into the details of semisimple Lie algebras, let us briefly
outline what our strategy will be in classifying their representations and what
structures we will need to carry out this strategy. We will look for commut-
ing elements HI' ... ' Hr in our Lie algebra that we will try to simultaneously
diagonalize in each representation. We should find as many such elements as
possible, and if they are going to be simultaneously diagonalizable in every rep-
resentation, they must certainly be diagonalizable in the adjoint representa-
tion. This leads (in basis-independent language) to the definition of a Cartan
subalgebra. The nonzero sets of simultaneous eigenvalues for adH 1 , ••• ,ad Hr
are called roots and the corresponding simultaneous eigenvectors are called
156    6 Semisimple Lie Algebras

root vectors. The root vectors will serve to raise and lower the eigenval-
ues of 7f(H1 ), ... ,7f(Hr) in each representation 7f. We will also have the Weyl
group, which is an important symmetry of the roots and also of the weights in
each representation. Finally, we will introduce the notion of positive roots,
in terms of which the notion of "highest weight" will be defined.
    One crucial part of the structure of semi simple Lie algebras is the existence
of certain special subalgebras isomorphic to 51(2; C). Several times over the
course of this chapter and the next one, we will make use of our knowledge
of the representations of 51(2; C). In particular, if X, Y, and H are the usual
basis elements for 51(2; C), then we will use repeatedly that the eigenvalues
of a(H) in any finite-dimensional representation of 51(2; C) must be integers
(Theorem 4.12). In view of the importance of this result, it is worthwhile now
to recall why this is so. From the Lie algebra point of view, we began with
an eigenvector for a(H) and then used a(X) and a(Y) to raise and lower
the eigenvalues for a(H) in increments of 2. Since the representation is finite
dimensional, this chain of eigenvalues must terminate in both directions. The
calculations of Section 4.4 (especially Lemma 4.11) show that this can happen
only ifthe highest eigenvalue m of a(H) is a non-negative integer. In that case,
all of the other eigenvalues of a(H) are of the form m - 2k and, so, are also
integers.
    From the group point of view, we recall that because SU(2) is simply
connected, for each finite-dimensional representation a of 51(2; C) ~ 5u(2)c
there is a representation E of 5 U(2) such that E (exp X) = exp a (X) for all
X in 5u(2). We note that 27fiH is in 5u(2) and that exp(27fiH) = I in SU(2).
Thus,
                 exp(27fia(H)) = E(exp(27fiH)) = E(I) = I.
This can happen only if the eigenvalues of a(H) are integers. After all, if), is
an eigenvalue for a(H), then exp(27fi).) is an eigenvalue for exp(27fia(H)) = I,
so exp(27fi).) = 1 and), must be an integer.


6.1 Complete Reducibility and Semisimple Lie Algebras

Recall (Section 4.10) that a group or Lie algebra is said to have the complete
reducibility property if every finite-dimensional representation of it decom-
poses as a direct sum of irreducible invariant subspaces. Recall also (Proposi-
tion 4.36) that a connected compact matrix Lie group always has this property.
It follows that the Lie algebra of a compact simply-connected matrix Lie group
also has the complete reducibility property, since, in that case, there is a one-
to-one correspondence between the representations of the compact group and
its Lie algebra. Since there is a one-to-one correspondence between the repre-
sentations of a real Lie algebra and the complex-linear representations of its
complexification, we see also that if a complex Lie algebra 9 is isomorphic to
the complexification of the Lie algebra of a compact simply-connected group,
                  6.1 Complete Reducibility and Semisimple Lie Algebras     157

then g has the complete reducibility property. In Chapter 5, we applied this
reasoning to 51(2; <C) (the complexification of the Lie algebra of SU(2)) and to
51(3; <C) (the complexification of the Lie algebra of SU(3)).
    In this chapter, we will study complex Lie algebras that are isomorphic
to the complexification of the Lie algebra of a compact simply-connected ma-
trix Lie group. As it turns out, such Lie algebras are precisely the complex
semi simple Lie algebras, which we now define. Although we will mostly be
concerned with complex semisimple Lie algebras, there is a brief discussion of
real semisimple Lie algebras at the end of this section.

Definition 6.1. If g is a complex Lie algebra, then an ideal in g is a complex
subalgebra b of g with the property that for all X in g and H in b, we have
[X,H] in b.
    Note that the definition of an ideal is stronger than that of a subalgebra.
For a subalgebra, we require only that the bracket of two elements of the
sub algebra remain in the subalgebra. For an ideal, we require that the bracket
of an element of the ideal with any element of g be, again, in the ideal. Any
Lie algebra g has two "trivial" examples of ideals: g itself and the zero ideal
b = {O}.
Definition 6.2. A complex Lie algebra g is called indecomposable if the
only ideals in g are g and {O}. A complex Lie algebra g is called simple if g
is indecomposable and dim g 2': 2.

    The term "indecomposable" is not a standard one, but since there does
not seem to be any standard term for this concept, I have invented one.
Note that the only indecomposable Lie algebras that are not simple are the
one-dimensional ones and that any two one-dimensional Lie algebras are iso-
morphic, since all brackets must be zero. A one-dimensional Lie algebra has
no nontrivial sub algebras and, hence, certainly no nontrivial ideals. Thus one-
dimensional Lie algebras are indecomposable but not simple.
    There is an analogy between finite-dimensional Lie algebras and finite
groups. Subalgebras in the Lie algebra setting are the analogs of subgroups in
the finite group setting, and ideals in the Lie algebra setting are the analogs
of normal subgroups in the finite group setting. In this analogy, the one-
dimensional Lie algebras (which are precisely the Lie algebras having no non-
trivial subalgebras) are the analogs of the cyclic groups of prime order (which
are precisely the groups having no nontrivial subgroups). However, there is
a discrepancy in terminology: cyclic groups of prime order are called simple
but one-dimensional Lie algebras are not called simple. This terminological
convention is important to bear in mind in the following definition.

Definition 6.3. A complex Lie algebra is called reductive if it is isomorphic
to a direct sum of indecomposable Lie algebras. A complex Lie algebra is called
semisimple if it isomorphic to a direct sum of simple Lie algebras.
158    6 Semisimple Lie Algebras

    Note that a reductive Lie algebra is a direct sum of indecomposable al-
gebras, which are either simple or one-dimensional commutative. Thus, a re-
ductive Lie algebra is one that decomposes as a direct sum of a semisimple
algebra (coming from the simple terms in the direct sum) and a commutative
algebra (coming from the one-dimensional terms in the direct sum).
    We will assume (in the spirit of this book) that the complex semisimple Lie
algebras we study are given to us as subalgebras of some gl(n; C). There is no
loss of generality in this since by Ado's Theorem every finite-dimensional Lie
algebra has a faithful finite-dimensional representation. In fact, for semisimple
Lie algebras, the adjoint representation is always faithful, as is easily shown.
(See Exercise 1.)

Proposition 6.4. A complex Lie algebra 9 is reductive precisely if the adjoint
representation is completely reducible.

Proof. An ideal is precisely an invariant subspace for the adjoint represen-
tation, as a moment's thought will confirm. So, if the adjoint representation
decomposes as a direct sum of irreducibles, then 9 decomposes (as a vector
space) as 91 EEl ... EEl 9m, where each 9k is an ideal and where 9k contains no
ideals of 9 other than 9k itself and {O}. Now, if X E 9k and Y E 91 (k =f. l),
then [X, Y] = 0, since [X, Y] must be in both 9k and 91 (because both 9k and
91 are ideals). This means that 9 must be the direct sum (in the Lie algebra
sense) of the 9k'S.
     Now, I claim that each 9k must be indecomposable when viewed as a Lie
algebra in its own right. After all, suppose that I:J is an ideal in 9k· Then, I:J
is also an ideal in 9. (The commutator of an element of 9k with an element
I:J will remain in I:J by assumption. The commutator of an element of I:J with
an element of 91, 1 =f. k, will be zero.) This means, by our assumptions on the
9k'S, that I:J = {O} or I:J = 9k·
     So, if the adjoint representation decomposes as a sum of irreducibles, then
9 is reductive. Conversely, if 9 is reductive, then 9 is a direct sum of inde-
composable algebras, which are then irreducible invariant subspaces for the
adjoint representation.                                                        0

Corollary 6.5. The complexijication of the Lie algebra of a connected com-
pact matrix Lie group is reductive.

   This follows from the above proposition and Proposition 4.36 (stating that
connected compact groups have the complete reducibility property). Note
that the Lie algebra of a compact Lie group may be only reductive and not
semisimple. For example, the Lie algebra of S1 is one dimensional and, thus,
not semisimple.

Theorem 6.6. A complex Lie algebra is semisimple if and only if it is isomor-
phic to the complexijication of the Lie algebra of a simply-connected compact
matrix Lie group.
                  6.1 Complete Reducibility and Semis imp Ie Lie Algebras      159

    I will not prove this result. Nevertheless, let us discuss the ideas behind it.
One direction is fairly easy, namely proving that if g is the complexification of
the Lie algebra of a compact simply-connected group K, then g is semisimple.
We have already shown that g is reductive, even if K is not simply connected.
Thus g = gl EB g2, with gl semisimple and g2 commutative. It can be shown
that the Lie algebra e of K decomposes as e = el EB e2, where gl = el + ie l
and g2 = t2 + it 2. Then K decomposes as KI x K 2, where KI and K2 are
simply connected and where K2 is commutative. However, a simply-connected
commutative Lie group is isomorphic to ]Etn, which is noncompact for n 2: l.
Thus, the compactness of K means that e2 = {O}, in which case g2 = {O} and
g = gl is semisimple.
    For the other direction, given a complex semisimple Lie algebra, we must
find the correct real form whose corresponding simply-connected group is
compact. For this, see Varadarajan (1974).

Definition 6.7. If g is a complex semisimple Lie algebra, then a compact
real form of g is real subalgebra e of g with the property that every X in g
can be written uniquely as X = Xl + iX2 with Xl and X 2 in e and such that
there is a compact simply-connected matrix Lie group KI such that the Lie
algebra tl of KI is isomorphic to t

    Theorem 6.6 tells us that every complex semis imp Ie Lie algebra has a
compact real form. The compact real form is not unique, but it is "unique up
to conjugation," as explained in Section 6.10.
    Note that K itself is not necessarily simply connected. Consider, for exam-
ple, the complex Lie algebra 50(3; q C gl(3; q which is the complexification
of 50(3). We note that 50(3) is isomorphic to 5u(2), which is the Lie algebra
of the compact simply-connected group 5U(2). This means that 50(3; q is
semis imp Ie and that 50(3) is a compact real form of 50(3; C). However, the
subgroup of GL(3; q whose Lie algebra is 50(3) is the group 50(3), which is
not simply connected. So, in this case, we have K = 50(3) and KI = 5U(2).

Proposition 6.8. Let g be a complex semisimple Lie algebra. If g is a sub-
algebra of gl(n; q and e is a compact real form of g, then the connected Lie
subgroup K of GL(n; q whose Lie algebra is e is compact.

Proof. The definition of a compact real form is that there is a simply-
connected compact matrix Lie group K I whose Lie algebra el is isomorphic
to t Let ¢ : tl -+ t c gl(n; q be a Lie algebra isomorphism. By Theorem 3.7,
there is an associated Lie group homomorphism <l> : KI -+ GL(n; C), and let K
be the image of this homomorphism. Since the image of a compact set under
a continuous map is compact, K is compact (and hence closed). Furthermore,
since the image of tl is t, Proposition 3.16 tells us that K is the connected
Lie subgroup of GL(n; q with Lie algebra t                                  0

    A corollary of Theorem 6.6 is the following result, which we will make use
of in the next chapter.
160    6 Semisimple Lie Algebras

Corollary 6.9. Every complex semisimple Lie algebra has the complete re-
ducibility property.

    This holds because the representations of 9 are in one-to-one correspon-
dence with the representations of K 1 , and compact groups have the complete
reducibility property (Theorem 4.36). Actually, it is not hard to prove (Exer-
cise 2) that among complex Lie algebras, only the semisimple ones have the
complete reducibility property. Thus, complete reducibility is sometimes taken
as the definition of semisimplicity for Lie algebras. For an algebraic proof of
complete reducibility of semis imp Ie Lie algebras, see Humphreys (1972).
    Up to now, we have considered only complex semisimple Lie algebras, since
these are the ones whose representations we will consider. (Working over C
instead of lR allows us to find nice bases for our Lie algebras.) Nevertheless, we
can define the terms ideal, indecomposable, simple, reductive, and semisimple
for real Lie algebras in precisely the same way as for the complex case.

Proposition 6.10. If 9 is a real Lie algebra and gc its complexijication, then
9 is semisimple if and only if gc is semisimple.

    I will not prove this result-see Varadarajan (1974). Note that the propo-
sition does not hold if the word "semisimple" is replaced by "simple." If gc
is simple, then 9 must be simple (since if ~ were a nontrivial ideal in g, then
~c would be a nontrivial ideal in gc), but the converse of this statement does
not hold. For example, it can be shown that the six-dimensional real Lie alge-
bra 50(3,1) is simple. However, its complexification 50(3,1; C) is isomorphic
to 50(4; C), which, in turn, is isomorphic to 51(2; C) EEl 51(2; C), and so the
complexification is not simple.
    As a consequence of the above proposition and Theorem 6.6, we see that
the real Lie algebra of a compact simply-connected group is semisimple. How-
ever, not every real semis imp Ie Lie algebra is of this sort. Consider, for exam-
ple, 51(n; lR). Of course, SL(n; lR) is noncompact, and there can be no compact
simply-connected Lie group whose Lie algebra is 51(n; lR), since such a group
would then be the universal cover of SL(n; lR), and the universal cover of a
noncom pact group is noncompact.
    Nevertheless, 51(n; lR) is semisimple, by Proposition 6.10, because its com-
plexification is 51(n; C), which is also the complexification of 5u(n), which is
the Lie algebra of a compact simply-connected group. So, a group G whose Lie
algebra 9 is real semis imp Ie should be thought of as being "almost compact."
This is to be understood not in any topological sense but rather in the sense
that G has a compact simply-connected "cousin" K with the property that
tc is isomorphic to gc. For example, SL(n; lR) has SU(n) as its cousin and
SO(n, k) has Spin(n + k) (the simply-connected double cover of SO(n + k)) as
its cousin for n + k 2 3.
    When working with finite-dimensional representations, one can always ex-
tend the representation to the complexification, and so it is easier to work
                 6.2 Examples of Reductive and Semisimple Lie Algebras     161

only with complex semisimple Lie algebras. This will be our approach in the
next chapter.
   There are several other equivalent characterizations of semis imp Ie Lie al-
gebras, for example, that the Lie algebra have no nonzero solvable ideals or
that the Killing form (B(X, Y) = trace(adxad y )) be nondegenerate.


6.2 Examples of Reductive and Semis imp Ie Lie Algebras

Let us consider some examples of Lie algebras that are reductive or semisimple,
starting with the complex case. The following table lists the complex Lie
algebras that we have encountered in this book that are either reductive or
semisimple. An entry of "reductive" in the table means actually "reductive
but not semisimple."

                         sl(n; q (n ~ 2) semisimple
                         so(n; q (n ~ 3) semis imp Ie
                         so(2; q         reductive
                         gl(n; q (n ~ 1) reductive
                         sp(n; q (n ~ 1) semisimple

To verify the results of this table, we use Theorem 6.6. First, sl(n; q is the
complexification of su(n), which is the Lie algebra of the compact simply-
connected group SU(n). Next, so(n; q is the complexification of so(n), which
is the Lie algebra of the compact group SO(n). Unfortunately, SO(n) is not
simply connected. However, so(n) is also the Lie algebra of Spin(n), which is
compact and simply connected for all n ~ 3 (Brocker and tom Dieck (1985)).
Meanwhile, so(2; <C) is one-dimensional commutative and thus reductive but
not semisimple.
    Next, gl(n; q is the complexification of u(n), which is the Lie algebra
of the compact group U(n). This means that gl(n; q is reductive. However,
the center of a semisimple Lie algebra must be trivial, and the center of
gl(n; q is nontrivial, containing all the multiples of the identity. Note that
gl(n; q ~ sl(n; q EB <C, where sl(n; q is semisimple and <C is one-dimensional
commutative. So, gl(n; q is reductive but not semisimple. Finally, sp(n; q is
the complexification of sp( n), which is the Lie algebra of the compact simply-
connected group Sp(n).
    All of the above-listed semis imp Ie algebras are actually simple, except
for so(4;q, which is isomorphic to sl(2;q EBsl(2;q. In Chapter 8, we will
discuss the classification of complex simple Lie algebras. It turns out that
every complex simple Lie algebra is isomorphic to one of sl(n; q, so(n; q
(n # 4), sp(n; <C), or to one of five "exceptional" Lie algebras conventionally
called G 2 , F4 , E 6 , E 7 , and Es.
    We now make a table of the real Lie algebras we have encountered in
this book that are either reductive or semisimple. Again, "reductive" means
162    6 Semisimple Lie Algebras

actually "reductive but not semisimple." In each case, the complexification of
the listed Lie algebra is isomorphic to one of the complex Lie algebras in the
above table. Note that there can be several nonisomorphic real Lie algebras
whose complexifications are isomorphic to the same complex semisimple Lie
algebra.
                        su(n) (n 2: 2)        semisimple
                        so(n) (n 2: 3)        semisimple
                        so(2)                 reductive
                        sp(n) (n 2: 1)        semisimple
                        so(n, k) (n + k 2: 3) semisimple
                        so(l, 1)              reductive
                         sp(n; lR) (n 2: 1)   semisimple
                        sl(n;lR) (n 2: 2)     semisimple
                        gl(n;lR) (n 2: 1)     reductive
    The other Lie algebras we have examined in this book, namely the Lie al-
gebras of the Heisenberg group, the Euclidean group, and the Poincare group,
are neither reductive nor semisimple.


6.3 Cartan Subalgebras

We now begin to develop the structure that we will use (in the next chapter)
in describing the representations of complex semisimple Lie algebras. These
same structures are used to give a classification of semisimple Lie algebras, as
discussed in Chapter 8. See Section 6.9 for how these structures come out in
the case g = sl(n; q.

Definition 6.11. If g is a complex semisimple Lie algebra, then a Cartan
subalgebra of g is a complex subspace ~ of g with the following properties:

 1. For all Hl and H2 in ~, [Hl' H2J = o.
 2. For all X in g, if [H, XJ = 0 for all H in ~, then X is in ~.
 3. For all H in ~, ad H is diagonalizable.

    Condition 1 says that ~ is a commutative subalgebra of g. Condition 2
says that ~ is a maximal commutative subalgebra (Le., not contained in any
larger commutative subalgebra). Condition 3 says that each adH (H E ~) is
diagonalizable. Since the H's in ~ commute, the adH's also commute, and thus
they are simultaneously diagonalizable. (It is a standard result in linear alge-
bra that any commuting family of diagonalizable matrices is simultaneously
diagonalizable; see Section B.8.)
    Of course, the definition of a Cartan subalgebra makes sense in any Lie
algebra, semisimple or not. However, if g is not semisimple, then g may not
have any Cartan subalgebras. (See Exercise 3.) Even in the semisimple case
we must prove that a Cartan subalgebra exists.

Proposition 6.12. Let 9 be a complex semisimple Lie algebra, let e be a com-
pact real form of g, and let t be any maximal commutative subalgebra of e.
Define f) C 9 to be f) = t + it. Then, f) is a Cartan subalgebra of g.

     Note that e (or any other Lie algebra) contains a maximal commutative
subalgebra. After all, let tl be anyone-dimensional subspace of e. Then, tl
is a commutative subalgebra of e. If tl is maximal, then we are done; if not,
then we chose some commutative subalgebra t2 properly containing t l . Then,
if t2 is maximal, we are done, and if not, we chose a commutative subalgebra
t3 properly containing b Since e is finite dimensional, this process cannot go
on forever and we will eventually get a maximal commutative subalgebra.

Proof. It is clear that f) is a commutative sub algebra of g. We must first show
that f) is maximal commutative. So, suppose that X E 9 commutes with every
element of f), which certainly means that it commutes with every element of
t. Then, write X = Xl + iX2 with Xl and X 2 in e. Then, for H in t, we have



where [H,XI] and [H,X 2 ] are in e (since e is a real subalgebra). However,
since every element of 9 has a unique decomposition as an element of e plus
an element of ie, we see that [H, Xl] and [H, X 2 ] must separately be zero.
Since this holds for all H in t and since t is maximal commutative, we must
have Xl and X 2 in t, which means that X = Xl + iX2 is in f). This shows
that f) is maximal commutative.
    We assume, as usual, that 9 is given as a subalgebra of gl(n; C). Let K
be the subgroup of GL(n; q whose Lie algebra is e. According to Proposition
6.8, K is compact. So, by the averaging method of Section 4.10, there exists a
real-valued inner product (-,.) on e that is invariant under the adjoint action
of K. This inner product can then be extended to a complex-valued inner
product on g, also denoted (.,.), that is invariant under the adjoint action
of K and that takes real values on e. This means that for each A in K, AdA
is a unitary operator on 9 (with respect to the inner product (-,.)). It then
follows that for each X in e, ad x : 9 -+ 9 is skew self-adjoint. (This is by the
same argument that shows that the Lie algebra of U(n) consists of skew-self-
adjoint matrices.) Thus, in particular, adH is skew for each H in t, and a skew
operator on a finite-dimensional complex inner product space is automatically
diagonalizable. (See Appendix B.)
    Finally, if H is any element of f) = t + it, then H = Hl + iH2' with Hl
and H2 in t. Since Hl and H2 commute, ad H1 and adH2 also commute, and,
therefore, ad H will be diagonalizable (since a linear combination of commuting
diagonalizable operators is diagonalizable). This shows that f) is a Cartan
subalgebra.                                                                     D

    It is possible to prove that every Cartan subalgebra of 9 arises as in Propo-
sition 6.12 (for some compact real form e and some maximal commutative
164    6 Semisimple Lie Algebras

sub algebra t of e) and also that Cartan subalgebras are "unique up to con-
jugation." (See Section 6.10 for more precise statements.) In particular, all
Cartan sub algebras of a given complex semisimple Lie algebra have the same
dimension. In light of this result, the following definition makes sense.

Definition 6.13. If 9 is a complex semisimple Lie algebra, then the rank of
9 is the dimension of any Cart an subalgebra.



6.4 Roots and Root Spaces

From now on we assume that we have chosen a compact real form e of 9
and a maximal commutative sub algebra t of e, and we consider the Cartan
subalgebra I) = t + it. We assume also that we have chosen (as in the proof
of Proposition 6.12) an inner product on 9 that is invariant under the adjoint
action of K and that takes real values on t

Definition 6.14. A root of 9 (relative to the Cartan subalgebra I)) is a
nonzero linear functional a on I) such that there exists a nonzero element
X of 9 with
                               [H, Xl = oo(H)X
for all H in I).
    The set of all roots is denoted R.

    The condition on X says that X is an eigenvector for each ad H , with
eigenvalue oo(H). Note that if X is actually an eigenvector for each ad H with
H in I), then the eigenvalues must depend linearly on H. That is why we
insist that a be a linear functional on I). (See Section B.S.) So, a root is
just a (nonzero) collection of simultaneous eigenvalues for the adH's. Note
any element of I) is a simultaneous eigenvector for all the adH's, with all
eigenvalues equal to zero, but we only call a a root if a is nonzero. Of course,
for any root a, some of the oo( H) 's may be equal to zero; we just require that
not all of them be zero.

Proposition 6.15. If a is a root, oo(H) is imaginary for all H in t.

Proof. By the proof of Proposition 6.12, there exists an inner product on 9
such that ad H is skew self-adjoint for all H in t. The eigenvalues of a skew
operator are necessarily imaginary and each oo(H) is an eigenvalue for ad H .
                                                                              o
    Note that the set of linear functionals on I) that are imaginary on t forms
a real vector space whose real dimension equals the complex dimension of I).
If t* denotes the space of real-valued linear functionals on t, then the roots
are contained in it* C 1)*.

   Suppose (3 = ka is a root (with k oj:- 0, since roots by definition are
nonzero). By Step 4, k must be an integer multiple of~, and by Step 5, k oj:- 2.
We may assume k > 0 (if not, replace (3 by -(3), in which case, k = 1/2, 1,
3/2, 5/2, 6/2, .... However, now (3 = ta, and precisely the same arguments
apply with a replaced by (3, and, so, 11k must also be an integer multiple of
1/2, with 11k oj:- 2. Of the possible values for k, the only one for which 11k
has these properties is k = l.
      Step 7. The root spaces 9a are one dimensional.
   Suppose not. Then there exists another element X' in 9a that is linearly
independent of Xa. In that case, X' is an eigenvector for ad Ha with eigenvalue
a(Ha) = 2. However, reasoning as in Step 5, if there is another eigenvector in
va for ad Ha with eigenvalue 2 (independent of X a), then there must also be
another eigenvector in va for ad Ha with eigenvalue 0, which we have seen is
impossible since the intersection of va with ~ is one dimensional.
    It remains only to show the uniqueness of the elements H a. Since the
root spaces 9a and 9-a are one dimensional, Ha is certainly unique up
to a constant. However, this constant is determined by the condition that
[Ha, Xa] = 2Xa , which is independent of the normalization of Xa since both
sides are linear in Xa. To say the same thing in a different way, the normal-
ization of Ha is determined by the condition a(Ha) = 2. This concludes the
proof of Theorem 6.20.                                                     0

6.6 The Weyl Group

We use here the compact group approach to defining the Weyl group, as
opposed to the Lie algebra approach. The compact-group approach makes
certain aspects of the Weyl group more transparent. Nevertheless, the two
174    6 Semisimple Lie Algebras

approaches are equivalent. See the comments following Theorem 6.33 at the
end of this section.
   We continue with the setting of the previous section. Thus, 9 is a complex
semisimple Lie algebra given to us as a subalgebra of some gl(n; C). We have
chosen a compact real form t of 9 and we let K be the compact subgroup
of GL(n; C) whose Lie algebra is t. We have chosen a maximal commutative
subalgebra t of t, and we work with the associated Cartan subalgebra I) = t+it.
We have chosen an inner product on 9 that is invariant under the adjoint action
of K and that takes real values on t.
    Consider the following two subgroups of K:

                Z(t) = {A E K IAdA(H) = H for all H in t},
                N(t) = {A E K IAdA(H) c t for all H in t}.

Clearly, Z(t) is a subgroup of N(t), and it is easily seen that Z(t) is a normal
subgroup of N(t). See Exercise 10 for an explanation of the notation. If T is
the connected Lie subgroup of K with Lie algebra t, then T c Z(t), since T
is generated by elements of the form exp H with H in t. It turns out that, in
fact, Z(t) = T. See Brocker and tom Dieck (1985).

Definition 6.28. The Weyl group for 9 is the quotient group W = N(t)/Z(t).
   We can define an action of W on t as follows. For each element w of W,
choose an element A of the corresponding equivalence class in N(t). Then for
H in t we define the action w . H of w on H by



As in the SU(3) case (Section 5.6), it is easy to verify that this action is well
defined (i.e., independent of the choice of A in a given equivalence class). Since
I) = t+it, each linear transformation of t extends uniquely to a complex-linear
transformation of I). Thus, we also think of Was acting on I). If w is an element
of the Weyl group, then we write w . H for the action of w on an element H of
I). It is easily seen that W is isomorphic to the group of linear transformations
of I) that can be expressed as AdA for some A E N(t).

6.7 Root Systems
In the previous sections, we have established several properties of the roots.
From Proposition 6.15, we know that the roots are imaginary on t, which,
after transferring the roots from 1)* to I) (as in Notation 6.24), means that
the roots live in it C I). The inner product (-,.) was constructed to take real
values on t and, hence, on t. The inner product then also takes real values
on it, since (iX, iY) = (-i)i (X, Y) = (X, Y) . So, the roots live in the real
inner-product space E = it.
    From Proposition 6.19 and Theorem 6.20 we know that the roots span it
and that if a is a root, then -a is a root but no other multiples of a are
roots. Theorem 6.27 tells us that for any roots a and (3, 2(a, (3) / (a, a) is an
integer. Proposition 6.29 tells us that the roots are invariant under the action
of the Weyl group, and Theorem 6.31 tells us that the Weyl group contains
the reflection about the hyperplane orthogonal to each root a. We summarize
these results in the following theorem.
Theorem 6.34. The roots form a finite set of nonzero elements of a real
inner-product space E and have the following properties:
 1. The roots span E.
 2. If a is a root, then -a is a root and the only multiples of a that are roots
    are a and -a.
 3. If a is a root, let Wa denote the linear transformation of E given by
                                                (a, (3)
                                Wa . (3 = (3 - 2-(- ) a.
                                                 a,a
      Then, for all roots a and (3, Wa . (3 is also a root.
 4. If a and (3 are roots, then the quantity
                                         2 (a, (3)
                                           (a,a)
      is an integer.
    Any collection of vectors in a finite-dimensional real inner-product space
having these properties is called a root system. The Weyl group for a root
system R is the group of linear transformations of E generated by the wa's.
We will look more closely at the properties of root systems in Chapter 8. Note
that Point 4 is equivalent to saying that (3 - Wa . (3 must be an integer multiple
of a for all roots a and (3.
    We have also established certain important properties of the root spaces
that are not properties of the roots themselves, namely that each root space
ga is one dimensional and that out of ga, g-a, and [ga, g-a], we can form a
subalgebra isomorphic to 51(2; <C).
    Finally, I claim that the co-roots Ha (as in Point 3 of Theorem 6.20)
themselves form a root system. Theorem 6.27 tells us that the co-roots satisfy
                                                        6.8 Positive Roots     181

Property 4 and Proposition 6.29 tells us that the set of co-roots is invariant
under the Weyl group and hence, in particular, under the reflections Wa'
However, note that since Ha is a multiple of a, the reflection generated by Ha
is the same as the reflection generated by a. Thus, the set of co-roots satisfies
Property 3. Properties 1 and 2 for the co-roots follow from the corresponding
properties for the roots, since each Ha is a multiple of a. The set of co-roots
is called the "dual root system" to the set of roots. See Chapter 8 for more
information on root systems, including many pictures.


6.8 Positive Roots
In the next chapter, we will classify the irreducible representations of 9 in
terms of a "highest weight." In the 51(3; C) case, we defined "highest" in terms
of the two roots al and a2. There is nothing sacred about those particular
two roots. What we need is simply some consistent notion of higher and lower
that will allow us to divide the root vectors Xa into "raising operators" and
"lowering operators." This should be done in such a way that the commuta-
tor of two raising operators is, again, a raising operator and not a lowering
operator. This means that we want to divide the roots into two groups, one
of which will be called "positive" and the other "negative." This should be
done is such a way that if the sum of positive roots is again a root, that root
should be" positive. There is no unique way to make the division into positive
and negative; any consistent division will do. The uniqueness theorems of the
next section show that it does not really matter which choice we make.
    The following definition and theorem shows that it is possible to make a
good choice.

Definition 6.35. Suppose that E is a finite-dimensional real inner-product
space and that R c E is a root system. Then, a base for R is a subset
.0. = {al, ... , a r } of R such that .0. forms a basis for E as a vector space and
such that for each a E R, we have



where the nj 's are integers and either all greater than or equal to zero or all
less than or equal to zero.
    Once a base .0. has been chosen, the a's for which nj ~ 0 are called the
positive roots (with respect to the given choice of .0.) and the a's with nj ~ 0
are called the negative roots. The elements of .0. are called the positive
simple roots.

    Therefore, to be a base (in the sense of root systems), .0. c R must, in
particular, be a basis for E in the vector space sense. In addition, the expansion
of any a ERin terms of the elements of .0. must have integer coefficients and
all of the nonzero coefficients (for a given a) must be of the same sign.
182      6 Semisimple Lie Algebras

Theorem 6.36. For any root system, a base exists.

   The proof of Theorem 6.36 is given in Section 8.3. In the case of sl(3; q,
one should verify that any pair of roots with a 120° angle constitutes a base,
but that a pair of roots with a 60° angle does not constitute a base. See
Chapter 8 for additional examples and pictures.

Proposition 6.37. If R is the set of roots of 9 relative to ~ = t + it and
if t. = {al, ... ,a r } is a base for R, then {Hcq, ... ,Har } is a base for the
system of co-roots.

      We will prove this in Chapter 8.

6.9.4 The Weyl group

Following the argument in the computation of the Weyl group of sl(2; q, we
see that Z(t) is the subgroup of K = SU(n) consisting of diagonal matrices,
and N(t) is the set of matrices A in SU(n) such that for each k there exists an
l such that Aek is a multiple of el. This means that associated to each A in
N(t) there is a permutation (k --+ l). So, the Weyl group is isomorphic to the
permutation group Sn and it acts on ~ by permuting the diagonal entries. For
each a = akl, the Weyl group element WOI. acts by interchanging the kth and
lth diagonal entries, and the Weyl group is generated by such interchanges.


6.9.5 Positive roots

Finally, we can find a base as follows. We take as our base the roots akl with
l = k + 1 (i.e., the roots ak,k+1). Recall that akl = Ekk - Ell. If k < l, we
note that



and, so,
                    akl = ak-l,k + ak-l,k-2 + ... + al,l+l·
Thus, every root akl with k < l can be written as a linear combination of the
simple roots with non-negative integer coefficients. (In fact, the coefficients
are either 0 or 1.) So, the akl's with k < l are the positive roots and the akl's
with k > l are the negative roots. Every negative root is just the negative of
a positive root and so can be written as a linear combination of the positive
simple roots with nonpositive integer coefficients.


6.10 Uniqueness Results

Given a complex semisimple Lie algebra g, we have made three choices: a com-
pact real form t of g, a maximal commutative subalgebra t of t, and a system
of positive roots. None of these objects is unique, and so it is important to
think about how different possibilities are related. Furthermore, we have only
considered Cartan subalgebras that arise as the complexification of maximal
                                                          6.11 Exercises    185

commutative sub algebras of a compact real form. It is not obvious that every
Cartan subalgebra arises in this way.
    The following theorems take care of all such worries. The following theo-
rems tell us that each structure is unique up to the adjoint action of relevant
group (G, K, or W).
    We assume that 9 is given to us as a subalgebra of some gl(n; C), and we
let G denote the connected Lie subgroup of GL(n; q with Lie algebra 9.

Theorem 6.38. Suppose that £1 and £2 are two compact real forms of 9. Then,
there is an element A ofG such that AdA(£r) = £2.

Theorem 6.39. Suppose that £ is a compact real form of 9 and that K is the
compact subgroup of G whose Lie algebra is t Suppose that tr and t2 are two
maximal commutative subalgebras of t Then, there is an element A of K such
that AdA(tr) = tz.

Theorem 6.40. If ~ is a Cartan subalgebra of 9, then there exists a compact
real form £ of 9 and a maximal commutative subalgebra t of £ such that ~ =
t + it. If ~1 and ~2 are two Cartan subalgebras of 9, then there exists A E G
such that AdA(~r) = ~2.

Theorem 6.41. Any two systems of positive simple roots can be mapped into
one another by the action of the Weyl group.

   In the last theorem, it should be understood that different orderings of
a1, ... , a r count as the same system of positive simple roots.
    I will not prove these results. Exercises 16, 18, and 19 provide proofs of
some of these results in the case 9 = sl(n; C).
    A related issue is the extent to which one can recover a semis imp Ie Lie
algebra from its root system. This will be discussed in Chapter 8. The result
is that if 91 and 92 are complex semisimple Lie algebras and the root system
for 91 is isomorphic (in the appropriate sense) to the root system for 92, then
91 and 92 are isomorphic Lie algebras. See Theorem 8.28.
