Representations of Complex Semisimple Lie
Algebras




In this chapter, we will study the finite-dimensional irreducible representations
of a complex semisimple Lie algebra g. These will be classified by means of
a "theorem of the highest weight." The theorem states that every irreducible
representation has a (unique) highest weight, that two irreducible representa-
tions with the same highest weight are equivalent, and that the elements that
actually arise as highest weights of irreducible representations are precisely
the "dominant integral" elements.
    Now that we have developed (in the previous chapter) the relevant struc-
tures for semisimple Lie algebras, most of the proof of the theorem of the
highest weight goes precisely as in the case of 51(3; C). Nevertheless, there is
one part of the proof that cannot be done the way we did the 51(3; C) case,
namely showing that every dominant integral element actually arises as the
highest weight of some irreducible representation. For this step, we need some
method of constructing representations, in contrast to the rest of the proof,
in which we assume that we are given a representation and we start analyzing
it.
    In the 51(3; C) case, we constructed the representations by starting with the
standard representation and the dual of the standard representation and then
taking subspaces of tensor products of these two representations. Although
similar methods can be used (as in Fulton and Harris (1991)) for other classical
groups, this method does not work in general.
    For a general semisimple Lie algebra, there are three standard methods of
constructing the representations. The first is a purely Lie-algebraic approach
using Verma modules. The second method constructs the representations as
representations of the associated simply-connected compact group and uses
the Peter-Weyl theorem and the Weyl character formula. The third method
constructs the representations as representations of the complex Lie group G
whose Lie algebra is g. In this approach, G acts on the space of holomor-
phic functions that transform in a certain way under the action of a certain
subgroup B of G. This approach is called Borel-Weil theory.
192    7 Representations of Complex Semisimple Lie Algebras

    We will give an essentially complete treatment of the Verma module ap-
proach. For the other two approaches, I provide a detailed outline with refer-
ences for further reading.
    In Chapter 8 we will work out examples for semisimple Lie algebras of
rank 2 and rank 3.
    We continue with the setting of the previous chapter. We consider a com-
plex semisimple Lie algebra 9 C gl(n; C). We choose, once and for all, a
compact real form e of 9 and a maximal commutative subalgebra t of e, and
we consider the Cartan subalgebra ~ = t + it of g. We also choose an inner
product on 9 that is invariant under the adjoint action of K c GL(n; C) and
that takes real values on e.
    We let R C it C ~ be the set of roots in the sense of Notation 6.24. We
choose, once and for all, a base ~ for R (in the sense of Definition 6.35),
the elements of which are called the positive simple roots. Every root is then
either positive or negative (with respect to ~) in the sense of Definition 6.35.
We let W denote the Weyl group, which may be thought of (Theorem 6.33)
as the group of linear transformations of ~ generated by the reflections w""
a E R. The set of roots has all the properties of a "root system," listed in
Theorem 6.34.
    We consider also the co-roots. For each a, there exist (Theorem 6.20)
X", E g"" Y'" E g_"" and H", E ~ such that [H"" X",l = 2X"" [H"" Y",l = -2Y""
and [X"" Y",l = H",. The element H", is unique (independent of the choice of
X", and Y"') and is called the co-root associated to the root a. According to
Section 6.5, the roots and co-roots are related by the formulas
                                          a
                                 H", = 2-(-)                               (7.1)
                                         a,a

and
                                                                           (7.2)

In particular, (a, H",) = 2. The set of co-roots also constitutes a root system,
and the set of H"" a E ~, forms a base for the system of co-roots.


1.1 Integral and Dominant Integral Elements

Definition 7.1. An element It of ~ is called an integral element if (It, H",)
is an integer for each root a.

   As explained in the next section, the integral elements are precisely the
elements of ~ that arise as weights of finite-dimensional representations of g.

Proposition 7.2. The set of integral elements is invariant under the action
of the Weyl group.
                              7.1 Integral and Dominant Integral Elements          193

Proof. Suppose that J1 E f) is an integral element and that w is an element
of the Weyl group. Then, since the inner product on f) is invariant under the
action of the Weyl group, we have for any root a, (w· J1, HOI.) = \J1, w- 1 . H Q).
Since the set of co-roots is invariant under the Weyl group, w-1·HQ is another
co-root (namely Hw-l. Q) and, so, \J1, w- 1 . HOI.) is an integer. This shows that
(w . J1, HOI.) is an integer and thus that w . J1 is an integral element.        D

   Checking that (J1, HOI.) is an integer for every root a is a rather tiresome
process. Fortunately, it suffices to check just for the positive simple roots.

Theorem 7.3. If J1 is an element of f) for which (J1, HOI.) is an integer for all
positive simple roots a, then (J1, HOI.) is an integer for all roots a and, thus, J1
is an integral element.

Proof. Suppose al, ... , ar are the positive simple roots. According to Propo-
sition 6.37 (which is proved in Chapter 8), H Q1 , ••• , HQ r form a base for the
system of co-roots. This means that for any root a, the co-root HOI. can be
expressed as a linear combination of H Q1 , ••• , HQ r with integer coefficients.
Thus, if \J1, HQj) is an integer for each j = 1, ... , r, then (J1, HOI.) is an integer
for all roots a.                                                                     D

   Recalling the expression (7.1) for HOI. in terms of a, we may restate The-
orem 7.3 as follows.

Theorem 7.4. An element J1 of f) is integral if and only if

                                       2 (J1,a)
                                         (a,a)
is an integer for each positive simple root a.

Corollary 7.5. Every root is an integral element.

    Recall now from elementary linear algebra that if J1 and a are any two
elements of an inner-product space, then the orthogonal projection of J1 onto
a is given by
                                       (a,J1)
                                       --a.
                                       (a,a)
Thus, we may reformulate the notion of an integral element yet again as
follows.

Theorem 7.6. An element J1 of f) is integral if and only if the orthogonal
projection of J1 onto each positive simple root a is an integer or half-integer
multiple of a.

    This characterization of the integral elements will help us visualize graph-
ically what the set of integral elements looks like in examples. (See Sections
8.5 and 8.6.) All of these reformulations of the notion of an integral element
194    7 Representations of Complex Semisimple Lie Algebras

should not cause us to lose sight of the "real" definition of integrality, which
is that (fL, Ha.) be an integer for each positive simple root a and, therefore, by
Proposition 6.37 for every root a. It is this form of integrality that explains
why the weights of a finite-dimensional representation of 9 must be integral,
as we will see in the next section.
    We now turn to the elements that will arise as the highest weights of
finite-dimensional irreducible representations of g.

Definition 7.7. An element fL of~ is called a dominant integral element if
(fL, Ha.) is a non-negative integer for each positive simple root a. Equivalently
fL is a dominant integral element if

                                     2(fL,a)
                                      (a,a)
is a non-negative integer for each positive simple root a.

    If fL is dominant integral, then (fL, Ha.) will automatically be a non-negative
integer for each positive root a, not just the positive simple ones.

Definition 7.8. The set of fL E it C ~ such that (fL, a) ? 0 for all positive
simple roots a is called the closed fundamental Weyl chamber relative to
the given set of positive simple roots.

    The dominant integral elements are precisely those integral elements con-
tained in the closed fundamental Weyl chamber. In the case of 51(3; q, the
fundamental Weyl chamber is a 60 0 sector-see Figure 5.2.
    We have observed that every root is an integral element. It follows that any
linear combination of roots with integer coefficients is also an integral element.
We can ask whether the reverse holds: Is every integral element expressible as
a linear combination of roots with integer coefficients? The answer in general
is no. This matter is discussed further in Section 8.10.


7.2 The Theorem of the Highest Weight
We continue with the notation established at the beginning of this chapter.
We begin with elementary properties of the representations of g.

Definition 7.9. Suppose 7r is a finite-dimensional representation of 9 on a

is called the multiplicity of the weight.

    To understand this definition, suppose that v E V is a simultaneous eigen-

number AH such that 7r(H)v = AHV. Since the representation 7r(H) is linear

eigenvector for all the 7r(H)'s and the vector {L is simply a convenient way of
encoding the eigenvalues. Note that the roots (in the sense of Notation 6.24)
are precisely the nonzero weights of the adjoint representation of g.
    It is easily shown that two equivalent representations have the same
weights and multiplicities.

Theorem 7.15 (Theorem of the Highest Weight).
 1. Every irreducible representation has a highest weight.
 2. Two irreducible representations with the same highest weight are equiva-
    lent.
 3. The highest weight of every irreducible representation is a dominant inte-
    gral element.
 4. Every dominant integral element occurs as the highest weight of an irre-
    ducible representation.

    The proof of the first three points of the theorem is almost precisely as
in the case of 51(3; C). The proof of Point 4 is substantially more complicated
than in the 51(3; C) case and is discussed at length in the following sections.
    It follows from Theorem 7.15 and properties of the Weyl group that ev-
ery integral element occurs as a weight of some finite-dimensional irreducible
representation of g. Specifically, if JL is an integral element then (Section 8.7)
there exists w E W such that JLo := W . JL is a dominant integral element.
Suppose V is the irreducible representation with highest weight JLo. Then by
Proposition 7.13, JL = w- l . JLo will be a weight of V.

 1. v is a weight vector with weight JLo

is either zero or a weight vector with weight {to - ai, - ... - al N , which is
lower than or equal to {to. So, {to is the highest weight for V.

terms with N = 0) and weight vectors with weight strictly lower than {to
(the terms with N > 0). It then follows froIl). Proposition B.14 that the only
weight vectors with weight {to are multiples of v, and, so, the weight space
with weight {to is one dimensional.                                                 0

Proposition 7.21. If 7r is an irreducible representation of fI, then the highest
weight J-lo of 7r is a dominant integral element.

Proof. We know already that all of the weights of 7r (not just the highest
weight) must be integral. Now, suppose that J-lo is the highest weight of 7r
and that v is a nonzero weight vector with weight J-lo. Then, 7r(Xa)v = 0 for
all positive simple roots a (otherwise, 7r(Xa)v would be a weight vector with
weight higher than J-lo). Now, consider the sub algebra sa = {Xa, Ya, Ha} of
fI, which is isomorphic to 51(2; C). Then, v is an eigenvector for 7r(Ha) with
eigenvalue J-lo(Ha ), and v is annihilated by 7r(Xa). By Theorem 4.12, this can
occur only if J-lo(Ha) is a non-negative integer. Thus, J-lo is dominant integral.

   We have now completed the proof of Theorem 7.15, except for Point 4,
namely that every dominant integral element arises as the highest weight of
some irreducible representation. The strategy we used to prove this in the
case of 51(3; C) does not work in general. We devote the next three sections to
a discussion of three different proofs of Point 4.


7.3 Constructing the Representations I: Verma Modules

We now turn to a discussion of the three standard methods of constructing an
irreducible finite-dimensional representation having a given dominant integral
element as its highest weight, namely Verma modules, the Peter-Weyl theory,
and the Borel-Weil theory. We begin, in this section, with the Verma module
approach. Given any J-l in b, we will construct a representation Vft called a
Verma module. ("Module" is just another word for a representation.) Here, J-l
can truly be any element of b, not necessarily dominant and not necessarily
integral. The catch is that the Verma module Vft is always infinite dimensional,
even if J-l is dominant integral. We will see eventually that if J-l is dominant
integral, then Vft contains an invariant subspace Uft such that the quotient
space Vft/Uft is finite dimensional and irreducible and has highest weight J-l.

7.3.1 Verma modules

The Verma module is constructed as follows. Let n+ be the subspace of fI
spanned by the root spaces fla where a is a positive root. This is a sub algebra
of fI since [fla, fli3l C fla+i3 and if a and (3 are positive roots, then fla+i3 is either
zero or is a root space corresponding to the positive root a + (3. Similarly, let
n- be the span of the root spaces corresponding to negative roots, which is
also a subalgebra. Then, fI decomposes as a direct sum (in the vector space
sense) of b, n+, and n-. Now, let J-l be any element of b. We want to construct
an (infinite-dimensional) representation Vft of fI having highest weight J-l. We
first describe Vft as a vector space and then describe the action of fI on it.

algebra g only-there is no associated representation of the simply-connected
compact group K. Although in the finite-dimensional case that every repre-
sentation of g comes from a representation of K, this result does not general-
ize to the infinite-dimensional case. What goes wrong is that in the infinite-
dimensiona~ case, the exponential of an operator may not be defined, because
the series defining the exponential may not converge in any reasonable sense.
We will revisit this issue in the next subsection.

7.3.2 Irreducible quotient modules
The good thing about Verma modules is that it is fairly easy to prove they
exist. The bad thing is that they are always infinite dimensional, even when
the highest weight /-t is dominant integral. The strategy for constructing finite-
dimensional representations is first to show that every Verma module has a
largest proper invariant subspace U/1 and that the quotient space V/1/U/1 is
irreducible with highest weight /-t. This much is true for any /-t in fJ. Then, one
shows that in the case that /-t is dominant integral, the quotient space is finite
dimensional.
    Let us look into this strategy in greater detail. The invariant subspace U/1
is defined as follows. It follows from (7.7) and Proposition B.14 that V/1 is a
direct sum of its weight spaces. Thus, for any vector V in V/1' it makes sense
to talk about the component of V in the one-dimensional subspace spanned
by Va, which we refer to as the va-component of v.

    If V is any vector space and U is a subspace of V, then one can form the
quotient space Vju. The construction of VjU is analogous to the construc-
tion of quotient groups, as described in Appendix A. We define two elements
of V to be equivalent if their difference is an element of U and then V jU
is defined to be the set of equivalence classes. Because U is a subspace, the
vector space operations on V (addition and scalar multiplication) "descend"
unambiguously to equivalence classes and make V jU into a vector space. If V
carries a representation of some Lie algebra 9 and if U is an invariant subspace
of V, then the action of 9 on V descends to an action on V jU and, thus, the
space V jU carries a representation of g, called the quotient representation.

7.3.3 Finite-dimensional quotient modules
The way we will prove finite dimensionality is to show that there is an action
of the Weyl group on VJL/UJL that transforms the weights in the same way
as in the finite-dimensional case. This will show that the set of weights for
VJL/UJL is invariant under the action of the Weyl group on b. Meanwhile, if /1
is dominant integral, then all of the weights of VJL/UJL must be integral (since
they are of the form in the right-hand side of (7.7)), and all the weights must
be lower than /1. However, standard Weyl group theory implies that there are
only finitely many integral elements A with the property that w . A is lower
than /1 for all w E W. So, if we can show that the Weyl group acts on VJL/UJL ,
then we will conclude that there are only finitely many weights in VJL/Uw
Since (even in the Verma module) each weight has finite multiplicity, this will
show that VJL/UJL is finite dimensional. (Note that the set of weights for the
Verma module itself is never invariant under the action of Won b.)
    How, then, do we construct an action of the Weyl group on VJL/UJL? Recall
that in the finite-dimensional case we exponentiate each representation 'if of
                   7.3 Constructing the Representations I: Verma Modules         205

action of the Weyl group is then obtained by restricting II to the subgroup
N(t) c K. In the infinite-dimensional case, the exponential of an operator
is not necessarily well defined (since the series for the exponential may not
converge) and, so, we cannot, in general, obtain a representation of the group
II. If J1 is dominant integral, then we will eventually conclude that V!-'/U!-' is
finite dimensional, but, of course, we are not allowed to assume that at. this
stage.

locally nilpotent operator must actually be nilpotent, that is, there must exist
a single k such that Xkv = 0 for all v. In the infinite-dimensional case, the
value of k depends on v and there may be no single value of k that works for
all v. If X is locally nilpotent, then we define eX to be the operator satisfying

    In the dominant integral case, the weights for V/1/U/1 are invariant under
the action of the Weyl group and all of the weights are integral (since the
weights that occur differ from M by an integer linear combination of roots). A
standard result from Weyl group theory (see Section 8.7) says that there are
only finitely many integral elements A such that W . A is lower than M for all
W E W. So, we conclude that if M is dominant integral, then there are only
finitely many weights in V/1/UI1'
    Meanwhile, for any M E ~, we know that V/1/U/1 has at least one weight

as we have shown, V/1/U/1 is irreducible, the same argument as in the finite-

Furthermore, all of the weights for V/1' and so also for V/1/U/1' have finite
multiplicity.
    We conclude, then, that in the dominant integral case, V/1/U/1 is the direct
sum of its weight spaces, there are only finitely many of these weight spaces,
and each of the weight spaces has finite dimension. This shows that V/1/U/1 is
finite dimensional and establishes that each dominant integral element arises
as the highest weight of a finite-dimensional irreducible representation of g.

7.4 Constructing the Representations II: The
Peter-Weyl Theorem

In this approach, we construct the representations as representations of the
simply-connected compact group K whose complexified Lie algebra is g. We
make use of the Haar measure on K, used already in the proof of complete
reducibility. (See Section C.4.) The Haar measure is a finite measure on K that
is invariant under the left and right actions of K. We normalize the measure
so that p,(K) = L We then consider the Hilbert space L2(K, p,) consisting of
measurable complex-valued functions f on K with the property that




The finite-dimensional irreducible representations of K will ultimately be re-
alized as certain finite-dimensional subspaces of L2(K, p,). The construction of
210    7 Representations of Complex Semisimple Lie Algebras

the representations is based on three main results: the Peter-Weyl theorem,
the Weyl character formula, and the Weyl integral formula. (See Section 7.6
for more information on some of these results.)

7.4.1 The Peter-Weyl theorem

In this subsection, it is not necessary that K be a compact Lie group; any
compact topological group will do. We consider the Hilbert space L2(K, f..L),
the space of measurable functions on K that are square-integrable with respect
to the Haar measure on K.
    The Peter-Weyl theorem gives a decomposition of L2(K, f..L) into finite-
dimensional subspaces that are invariant under the left and right actions of
K. Specifically, if ~ is a finite-dimensional irreducible representation of K
acting on a vector space V, then we consider the space of matrix entries of
~. Suppose we choose a basis {ud for V. Then, for each x E K, the linear
operator ~(x) can be expressed as a matrix with respect to this basis; we
denote the entries of this matrix as ~(X)kl' Then, a matrix entry for ~ is a
function on K that can be expressed in the form

    The significance of the space of matrix entries is that it is a finite-

for all x and y in K. Any function f on K satisfying f(xyx- 1 ) = f(y) for all
x and y in K is called a class function. The reason for this terminology is
that the set of group elements of the form xyx- 1 , with y E K fixed and x
ranging over K, is called the conjugacy class of y. A class function is then
a function that is constant on each conjugacy class.
    It is easily verified that two equivalent representations have the same char-
acter. The converse of this is much less obvious but also true: If two finite-
dimensional representations of K have the same character, they are equivalent.
    The Peter-Weyl theorem gives a way of expressing any function f E
L2(K, /-l) in a series expansion in terms of matrix entries of the irreducible
representations of K. We are interested primarily in the case in which f is a
class function. In that case, the expansion involves only the characters. The
Peter-Weyl theorem, specialized to the case of class functions, is as follows.

Theorem 7.27 (Peter-Weyl). Let L2(K, /-l)K denote the subspace of the
Hilbert space L2(K, /-l) consisting of square-integrable class functions. Then,
the functions

form an orthonormal basis for L2(K, /-l)K, where ~ ranges over the equivalence
classes of irreducible finite-dimensional representations of K.

   Proving that the characters form an orthonormal set of functions in
L2(K, /-l)K is a fairly elementary calculation using lit·tle more than Schur's
Lemma. (See Section II.4 of Brocker and tom Dieck (1985).) Proving that the
characters form an orthonormal basis for L2(K, /-l)K requires some analytical
argument. (See Section III.3 of Brocker and tom Dieck (1985).)

7.4.2 The Weyl character formula

We now assume that K is a simply-connected compact Lie group. (There is
also a version of the result for connected compact Lie groups that are not
simply connected.) We choose, as usual, a maximal commutative subalgebra
t of t and we let T be the connected Lie subgroup of K whose Lie algebra is t.
It can be shown that T is a closed subgroup of K (called a "maximal torus").
It can further be shown that every element of K is conjugate to an element
212     7 Representations of Complex Semisimple Lie Algebras

of T. This means that the values of a class function on K are, in principle,
determined by its values on T. The Weyl character formula is a formula for
the restriction to T of the character of an irreducible representation of K.
    We let 9 denote the complexification of the Lie algebra e of K, so that 9
is a complex semis imp Ie Lie algebra. Then, ~ := t + it is a Cartan sub algebra
in g. We follow Notation 6.24 and regard the roots as elements of ~ (not ~*).
Proposition 6.15 (expressed in terms of Notation 6.24) tells us that if a E ~
is a root, then (a, H) is imaginary for all H in t, which means that a itself is
in it. It is then convenient to introduce the real roots, which are simply Iii
times the ordinary roots. This means that a real root is a nonzero element a
of t with the property that there exists a nonzero X in 9 with

                               [H, Xl = i(a, H)X

for all H in t (or, equivalently, for all H in ~). We can also introduce the real
co-roots as the elements of t of the form Ha = 2al (a, a), where a is a real
root.
    In the same way, we will consider the real weights, which we think of as
elements of t in the same way as for the roots. So, if (~, V) is an irreducible
representation, then an element IL of t is called a real weight for ~ if there
exists a nonzero vector v E V such that

                               a(H)v = i(IL, H)v

for all H in t. (Here, a is the Lie algebra representation associated to the
group representation ~.) An element IL of t is said to be integral if (IL, Ha)
is an integer for each real co-root Ha. (All of the "real" objects are simply
Iii times the corresponding objects without the qualifier "real.") The real
weights of any finite-dimensional representation of 9 must be integral.
    For the rest of this section, all of roots and weights will be assumed real,
even if this is not explicitly stated.
    If a is an integral element, then it can be shown that there is a function
f on T satisfying
                                                                          (7.22)
for all H E ~. To understand this assertion, note that because T is connected
and commutative, every element t of T can be expressed as t = e H (Exercise
25 from Chapter 2). However, a given t can be expressed as t = e H in many
different ways; the content of the above assertion is that the right side of (7.22)
is independent of the choice of H for a given t. This means that we want to
say that the right-hand side of (7.22) defines a function on T, not just on t.
We will discuss this point further in the next subsection.
    Next, we introduce the element 6 of t defined to be half the sum of the
positive roots:

                                       1

                                           aER+

It can be shown that 0 is an integral element. (Clearly, 20 is integral, but it is
not obvious that 0 itself is integral.) Finally, if w is any element of the Weyl
group, we think of w as an orthogonal linear transformation of t~in which
case, det(w) = ±1.
    We are now ready to state the Weyl character formula.

Theorem 7.28 (Weyl Character Formula). If E is an irreducible repre-
sentation of K with highest real weight J.L, then we have

for all H in t for which the denominator of the right-hand side of (7.23) is

    The set of points H for which the denominator of the Weyl character
formula (the so-called Weyl denominator) is nonzero is dense in t. At points
where the denominator is zero, there is an apparent singularity in the formula
for x~. However, actually at such points the numerator is also zero and the
character itself remains finite (as must be the case since, from the definition
of the character, it is well defined and finite at every point). Note that the
character formula gives a formula for the restriction of X~ to T. Since X~ is a
class function and since (as we have asserted but not proved) every element of
K is conjugate to an element of T, knowing X~ on T determines, in principle,
X~ on all of K.
    A sketch of the proof of the Weyl character formula is given in Section 7.6.

7.4.3 Constructing the representations

Recall that our goal is to show that every dominant integral element J.L actually
arises as the highest weight of some irreducible representation of K. To do
this, we consider an arbitrary dominant integral element J.L, which, at the
moment, we do not know to be the highest weight of any representation.
However, whether or not J.L is the highest weight of some representation, it
can be shown that the right-hand side of (7.23) defines a function on T that
is invariant under the action of the Weyl group. Then, there exists a unique
class function 11-' on K whose restriction to T is given by the right-hand side
of (7.23). Using something called the Weyl integral formula (see Section
7.6), it can be shown that the functions 11-" where J.L ranges over all dominant
integral elements, are orthonormal. It is essential here that we can prove that
all of the II-"s are orthonormal by direct computation, without appealing to
the Peter-Weyl theorem and without knowing that every J.L is the highest
weight of a representation.
    Let us take stock of the situation. We have the following results. First, the
Peter-Weyl theorem tells us that the characters for the (equivalence classes of)
irreducible representations form an orthonormal basis for the space of £2 class
214     7 Representations of Complex Semi simple Lie Algebras

functions. Second, the Weyl character formula tells us that for an irreducible
representation having highest weight fL, the character of the representation
is given by (7.23). Third, the Weyl integral formula tells us that if for every
dominant integral element fL we define 11" to be the unique class function whose
restriction to T is given by (7.23), then the II"'s are orthonormal. This holds
even though we do not know at the moment that every dominant integral
element is the highest weight of a representation.
     These results together imply that every dominant integral element is ac-
tually the highest weight of some representation. To see this, note that the
Peter-Weyl theorem and the Weyl character formula tell us that the set of
II"'s, where fL ranges over all the highest weights of representations, form an
orthonormal basis for L 2 (K,fL)K. On the other hand, the Weyl integral for-
mula says that the set of II"'s, where fL ranges over all dominant integral
elements, forms an orthonormal set. This second orthonormal set contains
the first one, since the highest weight of an irreducible representation must
be dominant integral. However, an orthonormal basis cannot be contained in
a strictly larger orthonormal set-if it were, it would not be a basis. So, the
only possibility is that the set of highest weights of irreducible representations
is equal to the set of dominant integral elements, which is what we are trying
to prove.
     To say the same thing a different way, suppose there were some dominant
integral element fL that was not the highest weight of any representation, and
consider the function 11"" Since (we are assuming) fL is not the highest weight
of a representation, the set of characters is a certain set of la's, where (j
ranges over some subset of the dominant integral elements not including fL.
However, the Weyl integral formula tells us that 11" is orthogonal to la, for
all (j -=1= fL· This means that 11" is a nonzero class function that is is orthogonal
to all characters, since the characters are all la's with (j -=1= fL. This, however,
is impossible: The Peter-Weyl theorem implies that any class function that
is orthogonal to all the characters must be zero. So, fL must, after all, be the
highest weight of some representation.
     To see this argument spelled out in greater detail, see Brocker and tom
Dieck (1985) or Simon (1996). The argument in those books is slightly more
complicated than the one described here because those books consider arbi-
trary connected compact groups, not necessarily simply connected.
     This "construction" of the representations of K is not very constructive;
that is, we have proved that a representation with each dominant integral
element exists, but we have not given a very explicit description of the rep-
resentation. If one looks at the proof of the Peter-Weyl theorem, one will see
that the representations are realized as certain finite-dimensional, translation-
invariant spaces of functions on K, but it is not especially easy to see precisely
which functions one gets. The Borel-Weil construction, described in the next
section, gives a more explicit realization of the representations. Thus, the
Borel-Weil construction is useful even if one has already proved that every
dominant integral element is the highest weight of some representation.
        7.4 Constructing the Representations II: The Peter-Weyl Theorem           215

7.4.4 Analytically integral versus algebraically integral elements

One step of the argument in the previous subsection deserves elaboration. We
asserted (see (7.22)) that if K is simply connected and /-L is a (real) integral
element, then there exists a function f on T satisfying

                                                                               (7.24)

for all H in t. Let us think about what is entailed in this statement. Since
the Lie algebra t of the connected group T is commutative, T itself must also
be commutative. It follows that the exponential map exp : t -+ T is a homo-
morphism. The image of this homomorphism contains a neighborhood of the
identity in T (by the local surjectivity of the exponential mapping for arbi-
trary Lie groups). Since, also, T is connected, it follows that the exponential
mapping for T is surjective.
    Now, let <P c t be the kernel of the exponential mapping; that is,

                              <P = {H E t le H = I} .

Since the exponential mapping for T is surjective, every t E T can be written
as t = e H for some H in t. If HI and H2 are in t and e H1 = e H2 , then (since t
is commutative) e H, - H2 = I and, so, HI - H2 is in <P. This means that every
t E T can be written as t = e H , and the H is unique up to adding on an
element of <P. So, now suppose we try to define a function f on T by defining
(as in (7.24)) f(t) = expi(/-L, H), where H is chosen so that e H = t. When is
this well defined (i.e., independent of the choice of H)? Well, if H is such that
eH = t, then any H' with eH ' = t must be of the form H' = H +¢, with ¢ E <P.
Therefore, we need that exp i(/-L, H) = exp i(/-L, H') = exp i(/-L, H) exp i(/-L, ¢).
This will hold precisely if (/-L, ¢) is an integer multiple of 27r. We conclude, then,
that the function in (7.24) is well defined precisely if /-L has the property that
(/-L, ¢) is an integer multiple of 27r for all ¢ in the kernel of the exponential
mapping. An element /-L of t having this property is called an analytically
integral element.
      For reasons of consistency with Appendix E, it is convenient to introduce
the set
                               A = {H E t Ie 2 71" H = I} ,
so that the elements A of A are precisely those for which 27r A E <P (i.e., those
A of the form A = ¢ 127r for some ¢ E <p). Saying that (/-L, ¢) is an integer
multiple of 27r is the same as saying that (/-L, A) is an integer. So, we have the
following definition.

Definition 7.29. An element /-L of t is called an analytically integral ele-
ment if (/-L, A) is an integer for all A in A.

   The following result summarizes the conclusion of the previous paragraphs.
216    7 Representations of Complex Semisimple Lie Algebras

Proposition 7.30. For fL E t, there exists a function f on T satisfying



if and only if fL is an analytically integral element.

    Meanwhile, we have another notion of integral elements, namely that fL E I)
is an integral element if (fL, He,) is an integer for each co-root He<. To distin-
guish this condition from the condition for an analytically integral element,
we call fL an algebraically integral element if (fL, He<) is an integer for all
co-roots. In the previous subsection, we asserted that (assuming K is sim-
ply connected) f in (7.24) is well defined provided that fL is an algebraically
integral element. In light of Proposition 7.30, this amounts to asserting that
every algebraically integral element is analytically integral. In fact, we have
the following result.

Theorem 7.31. If K is simply connected, then the set of algebraically integral
elements and the set of analytically integral elements are the same.

    This theorem is not at all obvious. It is not hard to show that every
analytically integral element is algebraically integral; indeed, this is true even
if K is not simply connected. Showing (in the simply-connected case) that
every algebraically integral element is analytically integral is more involved.
See Section E.4 for more information.
    Books such as Brocker and tom Dieck (1985) and Simon (1996) are con-
cerned with the representations of compact Lie groups, which are not assumed
to be simply connected. These books do not address the issue of whether every
Lie algebra representation comes from a group representation. Thus, in those
books, the only relevant notion of integral element is that of an analytically
integral element and one never needs to worry about the relationship between
algebraically integral and analytically integral element. The theorem of the
highest weight, as presented in those books, says that every dominant and
analytically integral element is the highest weight of a representation of K.
This result holds whether K is simply connected or not.
    We, on the other hand, wish to connect the compact-group approach to
the Lie algebra approach and, for this, it is necessary to know Theorem 7.31.

n ranging over some subset of the non-negative integers not including m. This
would mean, by (7.28), that 1m is a nonzero class function that is orthogonal
to all of the characters (since the characters are all In's with n =I- m). However,
the Peter-Weyl theorem says that the characters form an orthonormal basis
for the set of L2 class functions on SU(2), and thus a nonzero class function
cannot be orthogonal to all of the characters. This, then, is a contradiction
and m must be, after all, the highest weight of a representation.


7.5 Constructing the Representations III: The
Borel-Wei! Construction
The Borel-Weil construction, described in this section, is often described after
the theorem of the highest weight has been proved (using, say, Verma modules
to prove that every dominant integral element arises as the highest weight of an
irreducible finite-dimensional representation). In such approaches, the Borel-
Weil construction is simply an illuminating way to "realize" representations
whose existence has already been demonstrated. However, it is also possible to
use the Borel-Weil construction to prove the existence of the representations,
and that is the approach we will follow here.

7.5.1 The complex-group approach

We have seen already two approaches to constructing the representations: the
Lie algebra point of view, using Verma modules, and the compact group-point
of view, using the Peter-Weyl theorem and the Weyl character formula. We
now consider the complex-group point of view, using something called the
Borel-Weil construction. (There is also a variant of the Borel-Weil construc-
tion that uses algebraic groups instead of complex groups.)
    Before getting into the details of the Borel-Weil construction, we need to
establish some notation and at the same time make sure that we understand
the relationships between the representations of the various objects (Lie al-
gebra, compact group, complex group). Let 9 be a complex semisimple Lie
   7.5 Constructing the Representations III: The Borel-Weil Construction   219

algebra realized as a subalgebra of some Mn(C). Let e be a compact real form
of g and let K and G be the connected Lie subgroups of GL(n; C) whose Lie
algebras are e and g, respectively. It can be shown that both K and G are
closed subgroups of GL(n; C) and, hence, matrix Lie groups. Let us assume for
simplicity that K and G are both simply connected. (It turns out that G is
simply connected if and only if K is-see the last section of Appendix E.) In
this case, the representations of K (i.e., continuous homomorphisms of K into
some GL(N; C)) are in one-to-one correspondence with the representations of
e, which, in turn, are in one-to-one correspondence with the complex-linear
representations of g = ec.
    Now, since we assume that G is simply connected, every complex-linear
representation of g can be exponentiated to give a representation of G. How-
ever, not every representation of G arises in this way. After all, if II is a
representation of G (continuous homomorphism of G into some GL(N; C)),
there is no reason that the associated Lie algebra representation 7r should be
complex-linear. For example, consider the representation of SL(2; C) given by
entrywise complex conjugation:




(This is a representation (i.e., a continuous homomorphism of SL(2; C) into
GL(2; C)) because the complex conjugate of the product of two matrices is the
same as the product of the complex conjugates.) The associated representation
7r of 51(2; C) is given by the same formula as II, since the complex conjugate
of exp(tX) is exp(tX), where X is the complex conjugate (entrywise) of X.
Clearly, then, 7r is not complex-linear but rather conjugate-linear.
    We call a representation of G holomorphic if the associated representa-
tion of g is complex-linear. Since g is a complex subalgebra of Mn(C), the
group G is automatically a complex submanifold of GL(n; C) (Appendix C),
and it can be· shown that if II is holomorphic in the sense of the previous
sentence, then II is a holomorphic mapping of the complex manifold G into
GL(N;C).
    Assuming still that K and G are simply connected, we conclude that the
following objects are in one-to-one correspondence with each other:

                     continuous representations of K
                     real-linear representations of e
                     complex-linear representations of g
                     holomorphic representations of G.

Here, "continuous" in the representations of K is to emphasize that we allow
any continuous homomorphism of K into GL(N; C). There is one other class
of objects that is frequently studied: the "algebraic" representations of G. It
is not hard to see that every algebraic representation of G is holomorphic;
220    7 Representations of Complex Semisimple Lie Algebras

it is less obvious but still true that every holomorphic representation of G
is also algebraic. So, actually, the algebraic representations of G are also in
one-to-one correspondence with the above-listed objects.

7.5.2 The setup
We continue to assume that 9 c Mn(C) is a complex semisimple Lie algebra
with compact real form e and that G and K are the connected subgroups
of GL(n; q with Lie algebras 9 and e, respectively. We will assume that K
is contained in U(n). (This is a harmless assumption since, by the averaging
method of Section 4.10, there is an inner product on      cn   that is invariant
under the action of K, and we can make a change of basis that converts this
inner product into the usual one on C n .) Having made this assumption, we
will have that each X E e will satisfy X* = -X, where X* is the usual matrix
adjoint of X. It then follows that for any Z = Xl + iX2 E g, we have that
Z* = -Xl + iX 2 also belongs to g. From this it is not hard to show that for
any A in G, A* is also in G.
    Now, suppose that 7r is a representation of 9 acting on some space V, and
II is the associated representation of G. We can choose an inner product on
V so that II(x) is unitary for all x in K. In that case, it is not hard to show
that II satisfies

for all A E G. For A E K, this means simply that II(A*) = II(A- I ) =

and (b+)* = b-.

     The argument in the preceding paragraph proves Proposition 7.32 in the
case where p, is the highest weight of some representation, in which case,
p, is certainly integral. However, of course, only dominant integral elements
arise as highest weights and the proposition claims that X /L exists for any
integral p" dominant or not. Furthermore, even in the dominant integral case,
we are going to use the proposition in proving that every dominant integral
element arises as the highest weight of some representation, in which case,
we are not allowed to assume the existence of the representations in proving
the proposition. Nevertheless, it is the dominant case that we are mainly
interested in, and the reason that we are interested in the homomorphism X /L
is because of (7.32).
     Let me now sketch briefly the proof of Proposition 7.32. It follows from
the "polar decomposition" for G (see Section E.5) that G is simply connected
if and only if K is simply connected. Consider the restriction of X/L to the
maximal commutative sub algebra t of t, which is the linear map H --+ (p" H) .
Let T be the subgroup of K whose Lie algebra is t. Since t c b+, it fol-
lows that T c B+. If G is simply connected (and so also K), then Corol-
lary E.8 implies that there exists a homomorphismA/L: T --+ C* such that
A/L(exp H) = exp(p" H) if and only p, is an integral element. Now, if X/L exists,
then, certainly, A/L must exist, since in that case, A/L is simply the restriction
of X/L to T C B+. Thus, if X/L exists, then A/L exists and, so (by Corollary
E.8), p, must be integral. To go in the other direction, recall that B+ is never
simply connected, even when G and K are. (This is why not all of the homo-
morphisms of b+ into C give rise to homomorphisms of B+ into C*.) However,
one can show that the fundamental group of B+ is isomorphic to that of T in
a natural way. This implies that all of the difficulty in passing from b+ to B+
is already present in passing from t to T. So, if p, is integral, Corollary E.8
tells us that we can pass X/L from t to T and, therefore, also from b+ to B+.

7.5.3 The strategy

The idea of the Borel-Weil construction is to realize each representation as
the action of G on a certain space of functions on G itself. In order to under-
stand the strategy, let us first assume that we have a representation II of G
and see what sort of functions on G we get from this. Then, the Borel-Weil
construction will reverse this procedure: We will first construct a certain space
of functions on G and then build the representation II from these functions.
    Thus, let (n, V) be a complex-linear representation of g and let II be the
associated holomorphic representation of G. We continue to assume (as in
the previous subsection) that II satisfies II(g*) = II(g)* for all 9 in G. Then,
consider the matrix entries of II. These are the functions On G of the form

                             Fu,v(g) = (u, II(g)v) ,                       (7.33)
   7.5 Constructing the Representations III: The Borel-Weil Construction     223

where u and v are elements of V. Because II is a holomorphic representation
of G, these functions are holomorphic functions on G. For a fixed u E V, let

      r = the space of all functions of the form Fu,v,      v E V.         (7.34)

For any fixed u, the map v --+ Fu,v is a linear map, which (by the definition
of r ) sends V onto FU. If II is irreducible and u =1= 0, then it is not hard
to show (Exercise 4) that the map v --+ Fu,v is injective. So, for u =1= 0, r
is a finite-dimensional vector space that is naturally isomorphic (as a vector
space) to V itself, by the map v --+ Fu,v.
    Now, if F is any function on G and h is an element of G, define a new
function RhF by the formula

                             (RhF)(g) = F(gh).
The operator Rh is the "right-translation by h" operator and it is a linear
operator on the space of all functions on G. Furthermore, we compute that



So, the map h --+ Rh is a homomorphism and we may think of R as a repre-
sentation of G, acting on the (infinite-dimensional) space of all functions on
G. If we apply Rh to a matrix entry, then we have
       RhFu,v(g) = (u, II(gh)v) = (u, II(g)II(h)v) = Fu,IT(h)v(g).         (7.35)

So, evidently, the right action of Gleaves FU invariant and, thus, FU is a
finite-dimensional representation of G. Furthermore, if u is nonzero and II is
irreducible, then FU is isomorphic as a representation to V, since the map
v --+ Fu,v is an intertwining map, by (7.35).
     The conclusion is this: We can realize any irreducible holomorphic rep-
resentation of G as a space of holomorphic functions on G that is invariant
under the right action of G.
     Let us look as well at the left action of G on functions. Consider the
"left-translation by h" operator, defined as

                              (LhF)(g) = F(hg).
I leave it to the reader to check that Lhlh2 = Lh2Lh1. (For our purposes, it
is not necessary that the map h --+ Lh be a homomorphism. If, however, one
wants a homomorphism, then one merely needs to replace F(hg) by F(h-1g)
in the definition of Lh.) We compute that

                  LhFu,v(g) = (u, II(hg)v) = (u, II(h)II(g)v)
                            = (II(h)*u, II(g)v) = Fu',v,
where u' = II(h)*u. Thus, for typical h, Lh does not leave the space F U
invariant, since the value of u is changed. Recall that we have defined II in
such a way that II(h)* = II(h*), and so we can rewrite the above result as
224    7 Representations of Complex Semisimple Lie Algebras

                        LhFu,v(g) = (II(h*)u, II(g)v) .                   (7.36)

    Let us consider the case that u = uo, where Uo is a highest weight vector
with some highest weight f-L. Suppose that we take h = b, where b is an element
of B-, so that b* is in B+. Recall the homomorphism Xp. : B+ --+ C* defined
in (7.30) and recall also that (by (7.32))

                                II(a)uo = Xp.(a)uo

for all a E B+. Applying this with a = b* and substituting into (7.36) gives

                                                                          (7.37)

for all b E B- and 9 E G.
    What do we conclude from all this? Given any finite-dimensional holomor-
phic representation II of G, we can realize II as a space FU o of holomorphic
functions on G, where the space FUo is invariant under the right action of
G and where each element F of FUo transforms under the left action of B-
according to (7.37). Now, it turns out that every holomorphic function on G
satisfying (7.37) is actually an element of FUo. (This is far from obvious at
the moment.)
    The idea, then, of the Borel-Weil construction is this. We start with an
integral element f-L and we want to show that f-L is actually the highest weight
of some representation II. We construct the homomorphism Xp. : B+ --+ C*
described in Proposition 7.32 and we define a space Fp.o as follows.

Definition 7.33. If f-L is an integral element, let Xp. : B+ --+ C* be the homo-
morphism given by Proposition 7.32. Then, we define Fp. to be the space of
all holomorphic functions on G satisfying

                             F(bg) = Xp.(b*)F(g)

for all b E B- and all 9 E G.

    Recall that if b is in B-, then b* is in B+. Although the definition of
Fp. makes sense for any integral element, we are interested primarily in the
case in which f-L is dominant integral. (It turns out that if f-L is integral but
not dominant, then Fp. contains only the zero function.) In the case that f-L
is dominant integral, we want to establish the following results. (1) Fp. is
invariant under the right action of G. (2) Fp. if finite dimensional. (3) Fp.
contains some nonzero elements. (4) Fp., under the right action of G, is an
irreducible holomorphic representation such that the associated Lie algebra
representation has highest weight f-L. This will show that the dominant integral
element f-L is indeed the highest weight of some irreducible representation and
will give a "concrete" realization of that representation.
    If we can prove all of this and we let Uo be a highest weight vector inside
Fp., then we will have
   7.5 Constructing the Representations III: The Borel-Wei! Construction    225

                                  FJ-L = FUo,
where po is the space defined in (7.34). Note, however, that we are not
allowed to define FJ-L to be equal to FUo since we do not know at the beginning
that there is any representation IT with highest weight f-L. So, instead, we
define FJ-L to be the space of functions having the properties that we know the
elements of FUo should have.
     The hardest part ofthe Borel-Weil construction is to prove that the space
FJ-L is nonzero (i.e., that there exists a nonzero holomorphic function F satis-
fying F(bg) = XJ-Lo(b*)F(g), bE B-, 9 E G). Note that if we knew that f-L was
the highest weight of some irreducible representation, then functions of the
form (7.33) would be in FJ-L and, thus, FJ-L would be nonzero. However, we are
trying to use the Borel-Weil construction to prove that such a representation
exists, and so we are not allowed to assume this.
     Since G is a complex group and B- is a complex subgroup, the quotient
manifold G I B- is a complex manifold. (The group B- is not a normal sub-
group of G and, therefore, the quotient G I B- is not a group but only a
manifold.) The space FJ-L should really be thought of as the space of holomor-
phic sections of a certain holomorphic line bundle over G I B-. This point of
view allows a large amount of differential geometric machinery to be brought
to bear, especially cohomology and the Riemann-Roch formula. For exam-
ple, it can be shown that G I B- is a compact complex manifold. (Specifically,
G I B- is identifiable with KIT, where T is the connected subgroup of K with
Lie algebra t.) This implies that the space FJ-L of holomorphic sections is fi-
nite dimensional. This machinery is beyond the scope of this book and is not
necessary if all one wants is to prove the existence of a representation with
a given dominant integral element. For more information on the differential
geometric side of things see Pressley and Segal (1986), Duistermaat and Kolk
(2000), Knapp (1986), Knapp (1988), and the article by Eastwood and Sawon
in Bridson and Salamon (2002).

7.5.4 The construction
We continue with the notation established in Subsection 7.5.2. We consider
an integral element f-L E I), which, at the moment, we do not assume to be
dominant. We let XJ-L : B+ -+ C* be the homomorphism given by Proposition
7.32 and we consider the space FJ-L of functions on G defined in Definition
7.33. We begin with one important but easy result.
Proposition 7.34. For any integral element f-L, the space FJ-L is invariant un-
der the right action of G.
Proof. Suppose that F is an element of FJ-L and h is an element of G. Then,
for all 9 E G and b E B-, we have (by the associativity of the product on G)
          (RhF)(bg) = F(bgh) = XJ-L(b*)F(gh) = XJ-L(b*)(RhF)(g).
This shows that RhF is again in Fw                                            o
226       7 Representations of Complex Semisimple Lie Algebras

      We are now ready to state the main result of this section.

Theorem 7.35. Assume G is simply connected. Let f-L be an integral element,
let XI-' : B+ -+ C* be as in Proposition 7.32, and let FI-' be as in Definition
7.33. Then, the following results hold:

 1. If f-L is not dominant, then FI-' contains only the zero function.
 2. If f-L is dominant integral, then FI-' is nonzero but finite dimensional. In
    this case FI-" forms an irreducible holomorphic representation under the
    right action of G, and this representation has highest weight f-L.

    This construction of the representations of G is called the Borel-Weil con-
struction.
    Let us elaborate slightly on the meaning of Point 2 of the theorem. The
space FI-' is invariant under Rh (h E G) and is finite dimensional. It can
be shown that the map h -+ RhlF is a continuous map of G into GL(FI-')
and, thus, FI-' constitutes a finite-dimensional representation of G. Point 2
of the theorem asserts that this representation is holomorphic, meaning that
the associated representation of 9 is complex-linear. The statement that "this
representation has highest weight f-L" then means more precisely that the as-
sociated complex-linear representation of 9 has highest weight f-L.
    It is not feasible for me to give a complete proof of this theorem here. I
outline the intermediate steps needed and prove the ones that can be proved
easily. The main omission is that I do not prove that FI-' is nonzero in the
dominant integral case. (Recall that if one already knows that a dominant
integral element f-L is the highest weight of a finite-dimensional representation,
then it is easy to show, as in the previous subsection, that FI-' is nonzero.
However, we are trying to use the Borel-Weil construction to prove the ex-
istence of such a representation; to do so we must prove directly that FI-' is
nonzero.)

Lemma 7.36. If FI-' is nonzero and finite dimensional, then FI-' forms an
irreducible holomorphic representation under the right action of G and this
representation has highest weight f-L.

    We know that in any finite-dimensional irreducible representation, the
highest weight must be dominant integral. The proposition thus implies that
if FI-' is finite dimensional and nonzero, then f-L must be dominant.

Proof. Assume that FI-' is nonzero and finite dimensional. Then, FI-' forms a
representation under the right action of G. Because the elements of FI-' are
holomorphic and the right action of G on itself is holomorphic, it can be
shown that FI-' is a holomorphic representation of G and, thus, there is an
associated complex-linear action of 9 on Fw By complete reducibility of the
representations of g, FI-' decomposes as a direct sum of irreducible g-invariant
subspaces. Each of these subspaces contains a nonzero highest weight vector

Lemma 7.37. For all integral elements JL, FJl is finite dimensional.

    I will not attempt to prove this result here. The usual argument is to

7.6 Further Results
Although the main result about the representations of a complex semisimple
Lie algebra is the theorem of the highest weight, there are many other useful
results about the representations. This section presents some of these results,
mostly without proofs. We continue to assume the notation established at the
beginning of this chapter.

7.6.1 Duality
Recall from Section 4.7 the notion of the dual representation Jr* associated to
a finite-dimensional representation Jr of a group or Lie algebra. We know in
general that Jr* is irreducible if and only if Jr is irreducible and that (Jr*)* is
equivalent (as a representation) to Jr. In the case of representations of semisim-
pIe Lie algebras, we have the following result. (Compare Exercise 2 in Chapter
5. )
                                                        7.6 Further Results      231

Proposition 7.39. If'Tr is an irreducible finite-dimensional representation of
g, then the weights of'Tr* are the negatives of the weights of'Tr. Specifically, if
/L is a weight of'Tr then -/L is a weight of'Tr* with the same multiplicity as /L.
Proof. Let V be the space on which 'Tr acts, let /L be a weight of 'Tr, and let
VI-' be the corresponding weight space. We know that V is the direct sum of
VI-' and the weight spaces for the other weights a of'Tr. Now let ¢ be a linear
functional on VI-" and extend ¢ to a linear functional on all of V by setting
¢ to zero on all weight spaces Va, a =1= /L. Let us compute how the operators
'Tr*(H), H E ~, act on ¢. If v E VI-' then we have, by the definition of the dual
representation,
         ['Tr*(H)¢](v) = [_'Tr(H)tT ¢](v) = -¢('Tr(H)v) = - (/L, H) ¢(v).
If v E Va, with a =1= /L, then both ¢(v) and ¢('Tr(H)v) are equal to zero, and so
we still have
                            ['Tr*(H)¢](v) = - (/L, H) ¢(v)                  (7.47)
(both sides equal to zero). Thus, actually, (7.47) holds for all v E V and,
therefore, 'Tr* (H)¢ = - (/L, H) ¢. This shows that -/L is a weight of 'Tr*, and it
is easily seen that the multiplicity of -/L in 'Tr* is the same as the multiplicity
of /L in 'Tr.                                                                     0
      Now, we have classified representations of complex semisimple Lie algebras
by their highest weights. So it is reasonable to ask how the highest weight of
'Tr* is related to the highest weight of 'Tr. The answer is provided in the following
result.
Theorem 7.40. There exists a unique element Wo of W such that for each
dominant integral weight /L, Wo . (-/L) is, again, dominant integral. If'Tr is
an irreducible representation with highest weight /L, then 'Tr* is an irreducible
representation with highest weight Wo . (- /L).
    The proof of this result uses standard properties of the Weyl group (Section
8.7) and is omitted.
    If it happens that -I is an element of the Weyl group (which is the case
for some semisimple Lie algebras but not for others), then we have Wo = -I.
This holds, for example, for g = 50(5; <C), whose root system is "B2 ." See
Section 8.5. In such cases, WO' (-/L) = /L and, so, the highest weight of'Tr* is,
again, /L. Thus, in Lie algebras where - I is an element of the Weyl group,
every irreducible representation is equivalent to its dual.
    For 51(3; q, -I is not an element of the Weyl group. If we take the usual
base ~ = {aI, a2} for the root system of 51(3; q, then Wo will be the reflection
about the line perpendicular to the root a3 = al + a2. (Compare Figures 5.2
and 5.3.) If /LI and /L2 are the fundamental weights for 51(3; q (circled in Figure
5.2), then every dominant integral element is of the form /L = ml/LI + m2/L2'
Then, WO' (-/L) = m2/LI + ml/L2. (Compare Exercise 3 in Chapter 5.) In the
case of 51(3; q, a representation with highest weight /L = ml/LI + m2/L2 is
equivalent to its dual if and only if ml = m2.

7.6.2 The weights and their multiplicities

It is important to know not only the highest weight of a representation, but
all of the weights, along with the multiplicities of the weights. We begin by
looking at which weights occur and then turn to the multiplicities. For pictures
of the weights of various representations, see Section 5.7, 8.5, and 8.6. Recall
from Section 5.7 that the convex hull of a finite collection of points in a vector
space is the smallest convex set containing those points.
Theorem 7.41. Suppose that V is a representation with highest weight f.Lo.
Then, an element f.L E b is a weight of V if and only if the following two
conditions are satisfied:
 1. f.L is contained in the convex hull of the orbit of f.Lo under the Weyl group.
 2. f.Lo - f.L can be expressed as a linear combination of the positive simple roots
    with integer coefficients.
    Note that Condition 2 implies that f.L is an integral element, since f.Lo and
all of the roots are integral. On the other hand, there will typically be integral
elements f.L contained in the convex hull of the W-orbit of f.Lo that are not
weights of V. After all, if f.L is an integral element, then f.Lo - f.L is also an integral
element, but this does not necessarily mean that f.Lo - f.L can be expressed as
an integer linear combination of roots. See Section 8.10 for more on this issue.
    As discussed after the statement of Theorem 7.15, every integral element
occurs as a weight of some irreducible representation of g. In fact, given an
integral element f.L, it follows from Theorem 7.41 and standard results about
the Weyl group (Section 8.7) that there exist infinitely many inequivalent
irreducible representations of 9 for which f.L is a weight. See Exercises 8 and 9
in Chapter 8.
    The proof of Theorem 7.41 is the same as the proof in the 51(3; q case,
which is sketched in Section 5.7.
    We now turn to the matter of the multiplicities. In the 51(3; C) case, there
is a simple pattern to the multiplicities, described in Section 5.7. In the gen-
eral case, things are more complicated and there are two standard results
about the multiplicities: Freudenthal's formula and Kostant's formula, both
of which allow one, in principle, to compute all of the multiplicities in any
representations. Although carrying out these computations in a particular
case can be arduous, there exist computer programs that can do them. I
present here (without proof) Kostant's formula, which gives the multiplicity
of each weight directly. Freudenthal's formula gives a recursive algorithm for
computing the multiplicities that may be more computationally efficient than
Kostant's formula in high-rank examples. Freudenthal's formula is in Section
22 of Humphreys (1972) and Kostant's formula is in Section 24.
    Suppose now that A is an element of the root lattice (i.e., that A can be
expressed as a linear combination of roots with integer coefficients). Now, let
p( A) denote the number of ways that A can be expressed as a linear combina-
tion of positive roots with non-negative integer coefficients. If A is higher than
                                                              7.6 Further Results      233

zero, then there will be at least one such way; if A is not higher than zero,
then there will be no such way and p(A) = O. Consider, for example, the case
of 51(3; C), in which we have three positive roots: al, a2, and a3 = al + a2.
If A = 2al + 3a2, then we have

                   A = 2al + 3a2 = al + 2a2 + a3 = a2 + 2a3
and, so, p(A) = 3. More generally, every element of the root lattice for 51(3; q
can be expressed as A = kal + la2 for a unique pair of integers k and l. If
either k or l is negative, then p(A) = o. If both k and l are non-negative, then
p(A) = 1 + min(k, l).
Theorem 7.42 (Kostant). Suppose that V is a finite-dimensional irre-
ducible representation of a complex semisimple Lie algebra 9 with highest
weight J-lo. If J-l is a weight of V, then the multiplicity m/l- o(J-l) is given by

                 m/l-O(J-l) =   L det(w)p(w· (J-lo + 8) - (J-l + 8»,
                                wEW

where 8 is half the sum of the positive roots.
    Let us consider, first, the w = I term in the sum, namely

                         p((J-lo + 8) - (J-l + 8»   = p(J-lo - J-l).
Since J-lo is higher than (or equal to) J-l, J-lo - J-l is higher than (or equal to) zero,
and so this term in the sum is nonzero. If the highest weight J-lo is sufficiently
far from the walls of the fundamental Weyl chamber and J-l is sufficiently close
to J-lo, then it will happen that for all w i=- I, W· (J-lo + 8) is not higher than
J-l + 8. So, p( w . (J-lo + 8) - (J-l + 8» will be zero for all w i=- I, and in such cases,
m/l-o(J-l) = p(J-lo - J-l). (In the Verma module V/l- O' described in Section 7.3, all
weights J-l have multiplicity p(J-lo - J-l).)
     As an example, consider the representation of 51(3; q with highest weight
(0,3) and consider the weight J-l = 0 occurring in this representation. Figure
7.1 illustrates the computations needed to apply Kostant's formula in this
case. In the figure, black dots indicate the weights of the representations and
the vertices of the outer hexagon indicate points of the form W· (J-lo + 8), where
J-lo is the highest weight, (0,3). Of the six elements of the form w . (J-lo + 8),
only two are higher than J-l + 8, namely J-lo + 8 and Weq . (J-lo + 8). Since
det(w",J = -1, we obtain

                           m/l-O(J-l) = p(al + 2(2) - p(2a2)
                                      =2-1=1.
In this representation, all weights have multiplicity one.
    In the case of 51(3; C), it is possible to use Kostant's or Freudenthal's
formula to show that the multiplicities follow the simple pattern described in
Section 5.7. For other Lie algebras, the pattern of multiplicities can be more
intricate.

7.6.3 The Weyl character formula and the Weyl dimension formula

Let K be a simply-connected compact Lie group, let £ be its Lie algebra,
and let 9 = £e, so that 9 is a complex semisimple Lie algebra. Then, there
is a one-to-one correspondence between the continuous representations of K
and the complex-linear representations of g. Let t be a maximal commutative
sub algebra of £ and let T be the connected subgroup of K whose Lie algebra
is t. Recall from Section 7.4 that the character of a representation II of K is
the function XII on K defined by

                              XII(X) = trace(II(x)).

Recall also the Weyl character formula (Theorem 7.28) which gives an expres-
sion for the restriction to T of the character of an irreducible representation
with highest weight JL. (The statement of that theorem is in terms of the real
weights, which are Iii times the ordinary weights.)
    Note that the value of the character at the identity is equal to the dimen-
sion of II:
                           XII (I) = trace(I) = dim II.
This means that the dimension of the representation can be obtained by eval-
uating the character at the identity. Unfortunately, this procedure is not as
simple as it sounds, since the Weyl character formula can be taken literally
only at points where the denominator is nonzero .. At the identity, the de-
nominator of the Weyl character formula is zero and the whole formula is
of the "zero over zero" form. Nevertheless, it is possible to use a version of
                                                       7.6 Further Results   235

L'Hopital's rule to evaluate the character at the origin so as to obtain the
following result.

Theorem 7.43. Suppose that 7r is an irreducible representation of g with high-
est weight p,. Then, the dimension of 7r is given by

                          d'         I1aER+ (a, p,+ 8)
                           1m 7r =     I1aER+ (a,8)    ,

where R+ denotes the set of positive roots and 8 is half the sum of the positive
roots.

    Note that the denominator of this formula is a constant, independent of
p,. So, the dimension is a polynomial function of the highest weight, and the
degree of this polynomial is equal to the number of positive roots. The dimen-
sion formulas for the cases of 51(2; q and 51(3; q, which we have discussed
previously, are consistent with this. For 51(2; C), the dimension is m + 1, and
for 51(3; q, it is ~(ml + 1)(m2 + l)(ml + m2 + 2), reflecting that for 51(2; q
there is one positive root and for 51(3; q there are three.
    Let us now verify that Theorem 7.43 agrees with the results we have stated
earlier for the 51(2; q and 51(3; q cases. For 51(2; q, we think of the roots and
weights as being simply numbers (the eigenvalue of H), and the inner product
as simply the product. The one positive root a is then the number 2 (reflecting
that adH(X) = 2X), and half the sum of the positive roots is the number 1. So,
if m is the highest eigenvalue of H occurring in an irreducible representation,
the dimension predicted by Theorem 7.43 is (m + 1)/1, in agreement with
Chapter 4.
     For the case of 51(3; q, we note that




Let us normalize all the roots so that (a, a) = 2 (as in Section 5.6). Using that
normalization of the inner product, we have ml = (al,p,) and m2 = (a2,p,).
Letting a3 = al + a2, we have
                               1
                          8 = 2"(al + a2 + (3) = a3·

We then note that (aI, 8) = 1, (a2, 8) = 1, and (a3, 8) = 2. So, the numerator
in the dimension formula is

            ((al,p,) + (al,8)) ((a2,p,) + (a2,8)) ((a3,p,) + (a3,8))
            = (ml + 1)(m2 + l)(ml + m2 + 2)
236    7 Representations of Complex Semis imp Ie Lie Algebras

and the denominator is (1)(1)(2). Thus, the dimension formula in this case
becomes
                .       1
               dIm 7r = 2(m 1 + 1)(m2 + l)(ml + m2 + 2),

as stated in Chapter 5.

7.6.4 The analytical proof of the Weyl cha.racter formula

Suppose that K is a simply-connected compact Lie group with Lie algebra
£, so that 9 := £c is a complex semisimple Lie algebra. We fix a maximal
commutative sub algebra t of £ and we let T be the connected Lie subgroup
of K whose Lie algebra is t. It can be shown that T is a torus; that is, T is
isomorphic to SI x SI X ... X SI. Furthermore, it can be shown that every
element of K is conjugate to an element of T; that is, given A E K, there
exists B E K such that BAB- 1 E T. (See Chapter IV of Brocker and tom
Dieck (1985).)
    As in Section 7.4, we work with the real roots, which are the elements a
of t such that there exists a nonzero element X of 9 with

                               [H, Xl = i(a, H)X

for all H in t (and, therefore, for all H in f)). We consider also the integral
real elements, which are those elements JL of t such that 2(a, JL) / (a, a) is an
integer for each real root a. As discussed in Section 7.4, for each integral real
element JL, there is a function fl" on T such that

for all H in t. Functions of this form are called torus characters; they are in
fact the characters of the irreducible representations ofT, which are necessarily
one-dimensional since T is commutative.

Proposition 7.44. The set of torus characters form an orthonormal set in
L2(T, dt), where dt is the normalized Haar measure on T.

    Actually, the torus characters form an orthonormal basis, but the com-
pleteness is not relevant here. This result can be thought of as the Peter-Weyl
theorem for T (since on the commutative group T every function is a class
function), but it can also be proved directly. See Exercise 2.
    We now let 6 denote half the sum of the positive real roots:

    The next key ingredient is the Weyl integral formula. I state there just
the special case of the formula that applies to class functions; there is a more
general version of the formula that applies to arbitrary functions on K.

some t' in T, then (since T is commutative) the value of <I> does not change.
Thus, we may think of <I> as mapping T x KIT into K; in fact, it maps onto
K, because every point of K is conjugate to a point in T. We want to use
the change-of-variables formula to change the integral over K into an integral
over T x KIT.
    In the case of a class function, f (AtA -1) is independent of A, so the
integration over KIT drops out and we are left with just an integral over T.
The factor of la(t)12 is essentially the Jacobian of the map <I> , which enters
as a consequence of the change-of-variables theorem. The factor of IWI arises
because an element B of K can be conjugate to several different elements of
T. In fact, if A is conjugate to a point t in T, then A is also conjugate to any
point in T of the form w . t, for w in the Weyl group. (This is true because
if B is in N(t), then Ad B preserves t and thus also T.) "Generic" elements of
K are conjugate to exactly IWI elements of T and the map <I> is "generically"
IWI-to-one, and this is why we need to divide by IWI in the Weyl integral
formula. To say the same thing another way, each "generic" conjugacy class
intersects T in exactly IWI points and, so, when integrating over T, we are
"overcounting" the conjugacy classes by a factor of IWI. We then need to
divide by IWI to compensate for this.
238    7 Representations of Complex Semisimple Lie Algebras

    There are two important features of the Weyl integral formula that we
will use in the proof of the Weyl character formula. The first is the factor of
IWI which we discussed in the previous paragraph. This factor is extremely
important. It is instructive to check the normalization of the Weyl integral
formula by checking the formula in the case f == 1. Then, the left-hand side
of the integral formula is equal to 1, and the right-hand side is equal to
IWI / IWI since the terms in the Weyl denominator are orthonormal elements
of L2(T, dt). (The points w . 15, W E W, are distinct integral elements.) The
second important feature is that the Jacobian factor la(t)12 is the absolute
value squared of a very nice function a. Of course, a Jacobian is always non-
negative and, thus, has a non-negative square root. This non-negative square
root, however, may not be a nice function-for example, it may not be smooth.
(For example, the non-negative square root of the function x 2 is lxi, which
is not differentiable at the origin.) Here, the function a (which is not non-
negative) is a very nice function, not only smooth but also having a very
simple expansion in terms of torus characters.
    We now turn to the proof of the Weyl character formula. The main ingre-
dients are (1) the factor of IWI in the Weyl integral formula, (2) the precise
form of the function a, and (3) the Peter-Weyl theorem. We do not need
the full power of the Peter-Weyl theorem here. We need only that the char-
acters of the irreducible representations of K have L2 norm 1 with respect
to the normalized Haar measure on K. This follows from fairly elementary
 "orthogonality relations." (See Section 11.4 of Brocker and tom Dieck (1985).)
    We now start thinking about characters of representations of K.

Proposition 7.47. If II is a representation of K, then the restriction of the
character Xn to T satisfies

                            Xn(e H ) =   L    mJ1 ei (J1,H) ,

    This is a "formula" of sorts for the character, but rather cumbersome
because the number of weights involved gets larger and larger as the high-
est weight gets larger, and also because computing the multiplicities can be
complicated. For example, trying to compute the dimension of II from this
formula is not so easy-one would have to sum up all the multiplicities of all
the weights involved. By contrast, the Weyl character formula yields (with a
bit of effort) the simple dimension formula given in Theorem 7.43.

can be seen very explicitly in the SU(2) case. See (7.25) in Section 7.4.) Note
that the possibility of cancellation arises because of the minus signs in the
formula for the Weyl denominator and because the same torus character can
arise in several different ways in the sum.

squared of (lXII is the sum of the squares of the coefficients, and this must
equallWI. This means that there can be at most IWI terms in the expression
for (lXII in terms of torus characters.

cannot cancel out in (lXIIi all other terms must cancel out or the L2 norm of
XII would be larger than l.
